Hypocoercivity of Langevin-type dynamics on abstract smooth manifolds
Martin Grothaus, Maximilian Constantin Mertin

Abstract
. In this article we investigate hypocoercivity of Langevin-type dynamics in nonlinear smooth geometries. The main result stating exponential decay to an equilibrium state with explicitly computable rate of convergence is rooted in an appealing Hilbert space strategy by Dolbeault, Mouhot and Schmeiser. This strategy was extended in [ GS14 ] to Kolmogorov backward evolution equations in contrast to the dual Fokker-Planck framework. We use this mathematically complete elaboration to investigate wide ranging classes of Langevin-type SDEs in an abstract manifold setting, i. e. (at least) the position variables obey certain smooth side conditions. Such equations occur e. g. as fibre lay-down processes in industrial applications. We contribute the Lagrangian-type formulation of such geometric Langevin dynamics in terms of (semi-)sprays and point to the necessity of fibre bundle measure spaces to specify the model Hilbert space.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification

Primary 58J65;
Secondary 37A25

1.
Introduction

A huge amount of research is going on in the area of hypocoercivity, hypoellipticity and diverse analytic methods
to study long-time behaviour of degenerated stochastically perturbed systems.
Herein, we concentrate on a hypocoercivity method applied to (geometric) Langevin equations,
see
[
CKW12
]
for some background of these equations and physical or chemical applications.
For the hypocoercivity approach we think of Langevin equations
as evolution equations of Kolmogorov backward type.
Formulated as an abstract Cauchy problem in a Hilbert space
a clever choice of an entropy functional gives rise of a certain norm on this Hilbert space
measuring the desired exponential decay towards an equilibrium.
This is the fundamental idea by J. Dolbeault, C. Mouhot and C. Schmeiser
for a hypocoercivity strategy, see
[
DMS15
]
.
However, we use the Kolmogorov backward (hypocoercivity) setting
developed in
[
GS14
]
,
because our focus lies on SDEs.
It’s by no means clear how to apply their
2.2

in case of stochastically perturbed mechanics on an abstract position manifold,
since there are various approaches and terminologies:
Y. Gliklikh discusses Langevin equations on manifolds of Itô
-
type,
see
[
Gli97
, Section 17]
;
in his opinion the Itô formulation is the most natural one.
However, he rarely talks about generators
whereas by
[
WI81
, Theorem V.1.2]
one easily gets
the infinitesimal generators of certain Stratonovich SDEs.
Besides, in
[
WI81
, Section V.4]
it is explained
how the theory of diffusions on manifolds in terms of Stratonovich SDEs
as motion in the frame bundle is strongly connected to Itô’s stochastic parallel displacement.
V. Kolokoltsov uses a notion of stochastic Hamiltonian systems
to study a ‘curvilinear Ornstein-Uhlenbeck processes’ on the cotangent space,
see
[
Kol00
, Chapter 4]
.
Using other ideas from classical mechanics
one could investigate critical points of the stochastic Hamilton
-
Pontryagin action integral
see e. g.
[
BO09
]
, in particular
[
BO09
, Theorem 3.2]
.
This might be interesting from a computational point of view
and could be linked to the other formulations via local Lagrangian vector fields.
But Kolokoltsov’s approach is directly based on a certain generator –
another approach of this kind can be found in
[
Sol95
]
.
Kolokoltsov prefers local coordinate forms,
however he is aware of
[
Jø78
]

providing a construction of an ‘Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process’ in the tangent space in invariant form.
E. Jørgensen uses the McKean-Gangolli injection scheme,
see
[
jM69
]
and
[
Gan64
]
,
to construct his process projecting a process in the frame bundle
which is a slightly inconvenient state space for real life applications.

The term (classical) Langevin equation refers in the purely Euclidean setting
to the following system of equations:

(1.1)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathrm{d}x_{t}&=v_{t}\ \mathrm{d}t\\
\mathrm{d}v_{t}&=-\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu}}\Psi(x_{t})\ \mathrm{d}t+\sigma\mathbin{\circ}\mathrm{d}W_{t}-\alpha\mathbin{\cdot}v_{t}\ \mathrm{d}t,\end{aligned}

where
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}_{x}^{\text{d}}
are positions in a space
\mathbb{R}_{x}^{\text{d}}
and

v_{t}\in\mathbb{R}_{v}^{\text{d}}
are velocities
for all times
t\in[0,\infty)
respectively –
the spaces
\mathbb{R}_{x}^{\text{d}}
and
\mathbb{R}_{v}^{\text{d}}
are thought as
independent copies of
\mathbb{R}^{\text{d}}
.
The model parameter
\alpha
is interpreted as a friction parameter, similarly

\sigma
as a diffusion parameter –
both are nonnegative.
The potential
\Psi\colon\,\mathbb{R}_{x}^{\text{d}}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies certain (weak) regularity properties
and
W=(W_{t})_{t\in[0,\infty)}
is a
d
-
dimensional Wiener process.
In fibre lay-down applications,
where the Langevin equation is used as a surrogate model,
one additionally would assume that
the (Euclidean) norm of the velocities is 1 constantly, i. e.
v_{t}\in\mathord{\mathbb{S}^{\text{d}-1}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{v}^{\text{d}}
for all times
t
.

We follow the philosophy of describing a stochastic dynamic
via its Kolmogorov backwards generator in invariant form.
In
Section 3
,
we stay rather close to classical Lagrangian mechanics,
since the ‘space of velocities
v
’

\mathsf{Q}=\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}

serves as configuration manifold
1
1

1

E. g. in
[
MR99
]
, this is also called configuration
space
,
however we reserve this term for the configuration space as a model of multiparticle systems.
The configuration space formalism might become handy,
if one studies fibre lay-down models of multiple filaments at once.

over the ‘space of positions
x
’ namely
\mathord{\mathbb{M}}
,
and we consider an evolution in the velocity phase space

\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathsf{Q}}=\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}
,
so in a (double) tangent space.
Together with the fact that
Langevin-type equations are second order differential equations
this leads quite naturally to Ehresmann connections and semisprays;
both concepts are closely related on a purely geometric level.
We have been imbued with these ideas during the reading of works by I. Bucataru
e. g.
[
Buc12
,
BCD11
]
.
In turn, those are rooted in results e. g. by M. Crampin, J. Grifone or R. Miron.
Later on, in
Section 4

we talk about fibre lay-down models
and demonstrate how to geometrically implement an algebraic side condition
like normalised velocities.
For sake of completeness, we also mention
[
Bis15
]

wherein J.-M. Bismut talks about Langevin processes in terms of hypoelliptic Laplacians,
i. e. as a diffusion interpolating Brownian motion and geodesic flow.

A key achievement of this paper is that
we explicitly don’t need parallelisability of
\mathord{\mathbb{M}}
.
It’s a wide spread mistake to work in tangent bundles
treating them like trivial bundles,
even though it’s very well-known that
most of the spheres are not parallelisable –
e. g.
\mathord{\mathbb{S}^{2}}
as the Hairy Ball Theorem shows.
Indeed, since the works
[
Ker58
]
by M. Kervaire
and independently
[
BM58
]
by R. Bott and J. Milnor it’s known that

\mathord{\mathbb{S}^{\text{d}-1}}
is parallelisable exactly for
\text{d}\in\{1,2,4,8\}
.
E. g. E. Jørgensen was sensitised to the issue
as the last remark in
[
Jø78
, Section 1]
shows.
If our model fails to capture the geometry of general spheres,
we didn’t find a reasonable model.
For that reason, we abolish notation like ‘
(x,v)\in\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}
’
with position
x
and velocity component
v
completely.
Instead we will be very careful to always emphasise the bundle structure:
Throughout this paper,
we always denote by
\pi_{0}
a tangent bundle projection no matter what the base manifold is
and extract the information on the position from a tangent vector
v

via
x=\pi_{0}(v)
–
i. e.
\pi_{0}
serves as an accessor or ‘getter’ method.
In the absence of a product structure of tangent spaces,
we rely on the ‘almost product’ structure induced by the universal property of local trivialisation.
As the example of spheres illustrates,
this is not just a technicality to be wiped out crudely with embeddings into larger Euclidean spaces.

Note that our configuration manifold is going to be
\mathsf{Q}=\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}
–
or some sub
-
fibre bundle, see
Section 4
.
Therefore, we don’t need
\mathord{\mathbb{M}}
itself to be orientable.
The commonly used integration of differential forms on manifolds instead of functions
is extended by integration wrt. so-called 1
-
densities,
where the latter concept does not need orientability.
We won’t discuss this in
Section 2
,
but refer to
[
Fol99
, Section 11.4]
and
[
Nic96
, § 3.4.1]

as well as to
[
BGV92
, p. 34]
for the existence
and to
[
Nic96
, Example 3.4.2]
for elementary properties
of the canonical 1
-
density associated to the Riemannian metric.
Such a notion of integration on
\mathord{\mathbb{M}}
is absolutely sufficient for our purposes.
The integration by parts formula,
which enables us to use techniques related to generalised Dirichlet form theory in the first place,
has to hold not for integration over the position manifold
\mathord{\mathbb{M}}
,
but for integration over the configuration manifold
\mathsf{Q}
which will automatically be orientable.
Hence, we will just talk about a ‘Riemannian volume measure
\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}
’
on the Riemannian manifold
(\mathord{\mathbb{M}},\mathord{\mathrm{m}})
:
either there is some orientation and this measure is induced
by the canonical volume form
\mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}
,
or there is none and the measure is induced
by the canonical 1
-
density
\lvert\mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}\rvert
.

In
Section 5
we first prove existence of Markov processes
solving the SDEs (
3.1
) and (
4.2
) treated in the preceding sections.
By finding appropriate cores for the corresponding generators
\mathord{\left\lparen L,\mathord{D\left\lparen L\right\rparen}\right\rparen}

and proving m-dissipativity in
Section 3
or
Section 4
respectively,
we have a strongly continuous semigroup
(T_{t})_{t\in[0,\infty)}
generated by
\mathord{\left\lparen L,\mathord{D\left\lparen L\right\rparen}\right\rparen}

on the model Hilbert space
H=\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathsf{Q};\mu\right\rparen}
,
where
\mu
is the equilibrium distribution.
For a suitable test function
g\colon\,\mathsf{Q}\to\mathbb{R}

the assignment
u(t,\cdot)=T_{t}g
yields a solution
to the abstract Cauchy problem
\dot{u}=Lu
on the model Hilbert space.
Using the theory of generalised Dirichlet forms
we show that there is a
\mathsf{Q}
-
valued Markov process
(X_{t})_{t\in[0,\infty)}

which solves the
L
-
martingale problem,
has
\mu
as invariant measure,
is conservative,
and most importantly it’s properly associated to
\mathord{\left\lparen L,\mathord{D\left\lparen L\right\rparen}\right\rparen}
in the resolvent sense.
The latter means that the transition resolvent

\int_{(0,\infty)}\exp(-as)\operatorname{E}_{\mathord{\mathrm{Id}}}[g(X_{t})]\,\mathord{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}s)

is a quasi-continuous
\mu
-
version of the resolvent

\int_{(0,\infty)}\exp(-as)T_{t}g\,\mathord{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}s)

for all
a\in(0,\infty)
and functions
g\in H
.
Second, we show that these Markov processes are
\mathord{L^{2}}
-
exponentially ergodic
in the sense of
[
CG10
]
.

We summarise our main results as follows:

•

We heavily make use of rather geometric concepts like semisprays and Ehresmann connections
to formulate analytic problems and objects in invariant form.
Thereby, we choose a quite accessible Lagrangian-type approach to higher-order SDEs on manifolds.
Even though the geometric tools themselves are well-known,
they have not been used
to treat higher-order SDEs on abstract manifolds systematically.

•

The applications of the general Hilbert space hypocoercivity strategy
presented in
[
GS14
]
and
[
GS16
]

are extended to the case of a quite abstract position manifold.
During this course we see
that strong mixing with exponential convergence to equilibrium of the corresponding semigroups are features of Langevin-type equations
all across (finite-dimensional) smooth geometry with just view natural geometric assumptions.
The main theorems are
Theorem
3.2

and
Theorem
4.1
.

•

Among possible applications of Langevin equations on manifolds
we spotlight their usage as surrogate model of fibre lay-down processes
in industrial production of nonwovens.
See
Section 4
and in particular
Theorem
4.1
.
Compare these results to e. g.
[
GKMW07
,
KMW12
,
GS13
]
.

•

In
Section 5

we first prove in
Theorem
5.1

the existence of a Markov process solving the martingale problem for our Langevin-type SDEs,
namely (
3.1
) and (
4.2
).
Afterwards we deduce that these solutions are
\mathord{L^{2}}
-
exponentially ergodic in the sense of
[
CG10
]
,
meaning that the rate of ergodicity corresponds to exponential convergence of the semigroups.
In the end, we argue that the
\mathord{L^{2}}
-
exponential rate is even optimal.
See
Corollary
5.2
and the subsequent remark.

2.
Preliminaries

Before we give a brief recap on the general hypocoercivity method
and establish several geometrical tools,
we fix the assumptions on the position manifold.

Condition 2.1

(Position manifold
2.1
)
.

(M1)

general geometry:
Let
(\mathord{\mathbb{M}},\mathord{\mathrm{m}})
be a
real,
finite dimensional,
connected
Riemannian manifold
with
\text{n}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\dim(\mathord{\mathbb{M}})\geq 2
.

(M2)

completeness:
Let
\mathord{\mathbb{M}}
endowed with the intrinsic metric be a complete metric space
2
2

2

The intrinsic metric induces the same topology as given on
\mathord{\mathbb{M}}
originally.
This is a consequence of the very definition of a (topological) manifold
as in
[
Lee12
]

which we choose to use here.

.

∎

Note that following a common way of speaking,
the manifold as described in
(M1)
has trivial boundary
\partial\mathord{\mathbb{M}}=\emptyset
.
In this paper, we do not address boundary problems for sake of simplicity.
From an analytic point of view,
assumption
(M2)
is plausible in it’s own right
and necessary e. g. for a discussion of Sobolev spaces on noncompact manifolds,
see
[
Heb99
, Chapter 3]
.
By the Hopf-Rinow Theorem,
we have that
(M2)
is equivalent to geodesic completeness of
\mathord{\mathbb{M}}
.
If we additionally assume an orientation on a manifold
which satisfies
(M1)
and
(M2)
,
then it is well-known that
the usual Laplace-Beltrami operator on smooth test functions is essentially self-adjoint.
See
[
Gaf51
,
Gaf54a
,
Gaf54b
]

for generalisations of the standard case on compact manifolds
as well as
[
jLM89
, Section II.5]

for general statements on so-called Dirac operators on spinors.
Moreover, we also want to recommend
[
Wol73
]
.
If we would assume nontrivial boundary,
then essentially self-adjointness of the Laplace-Beltrami operator is rather delicate.
This challenging problem has been tackled in
[
Pé13
]

and the noteworthy paper
[
ILP15
]
.

2.1.
General hypocoercivity method

At this point, we want to give an almost criminally brief overview of the hypocoercivity method
in the abstract Hilbert space setting.
If the reader is familiar with this topic,
this section just clarifies some notation.

Originally, the method was algebraically developed
by J. Dolbeault, C. Mouhot and C. Schmeiser
in
[
DMS09
]
and
[
DMS15
]

studying linear kinetic equations –
algebraically in the sense that
issues of operator domains have been neglected.
Substantial contributions on the general hypocoercivity approach can be found
in
[
Vil07
]
and
[
Vil09
]
–
e. g. the oncoming condition
(P3)
on the potentials Hessian appears in
[
Vil09
, Theorem 35]
first.
Our main reference is
[
GS14
]
for two reasons.
On conceptional side,
the authors really tackled the long-time behaviour of solutions of the SDE
via the Kolmogorov backward setting
instead of investigating the Fokker-Planck equation.
In the case of Euclidean position space, normalised velocity,
and the invariant measure having a density wrt. Lebesgue measure
there is an isometric isomorphism the Kolmogorov backward to the Fokker-Planck setting,
see
[
GMS12
]
.
However, in a general manifold it is not clear how to map between these two settings.
On technical side,
to gain a rigorous proof of the
2.2

and to check its assumptions in applications
several domain issues have to be taken into account,
which are often times just omitted.
For broader discussion and more detailed explanations
we also refer to
[
Sti14
]
.
The main result, we aim to apply to our fibre lay-down model,
tells us what microstructure we have to expect in the fleece:
The faster the convergence to the equilibrium state
the more uniform the nonwoven material will appear.
The formal result reads as follows.

Theorem 2.2

(Hypocoercivity Theorem)
.

Assume the conditions
2.3
as well as
2.4
(as given below)
and denote by
(T_{t})_{t\in[0,\infty)}

the operator semigroup generated by
L
on the Hilbert space
H
.

Then, there exist constants
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\in(0,\infty)

computable in terms of the constants
\Lambda_{m}
,
\Lambda_{M}
,
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
appearing in the assumptions
such that it holds

\lVert T_{t}g-\left\lparen g\mathbin{,}1\right\rparen_{H}\rVert_{H}\leq\kappa_{1}\mathord{\mathrm{e}}^{-\kappa_{2}t}\lVert g-\left\lparen g\mathbin{,}1\right\rparen_{H}\rVert_{H}\qquad\text{for all times }t\geq 0

and for all
g\in H
.

Proof.

See
[
GS14
, Theorem 2.18]
;
in its proof one learns
how to compute the constants
\kappa_{1}
and
\kappa_{2}
.
∎

Now, we are going to explain what the sets of conditions
2.3
and
2.4
are.
We just mention as a fact that there are more general data assumptions such that
the
2.2
is still valid,
see
[
Sti14
, Section 2.2.3]
.

Condition 2.3

(Data conditions
2.3
)
.

(D1)

model Hilbert space:
Let
(E,\mathfrak{E},\mu)
be a probability space and
define the Hilbert space
H
to be
\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen E;\mu\right\rparen}=\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mu\right\rparen}
.

(D2)

Strongly continuous semigroup and its infinitesimal generator:
Let
(L,\mathord{D\left\lparen L\right\rparen})
be a linear operator on
H
and

(T_{t})_{t\in[0,\infty)}
be the strongly continuous semigroup generated by
L
, i. e.
T_{0}=\mathord{\mathrm{Id}}_{H}
and
T_{t}f\rightarrow f
as
t\downarrow 0
for all
f\in H
.

(D3)

Core property:
Let
D\subseteq\mathord{D\left\lparen L\right\rparen}
be dense in
H
and
an operator core of
(L,\mathord{D\left\lparen L\right\rparen})
, i. e. the closure of
(L,D)
coincides with
(L,\mathord{D\left\lparen L\right\rparen})
.

(D4)

SAD-Decomposition of generator into symmetric and antisymmetric part:
Let
(S,\mathord{D\left\lparen S\right\rparen})
be symmetric and
let
(A,\mathord{D\left\lparen A\right\rparen})
be closed and antisymmetric on
H
s. t.
D\subseteq\mathord{D\left\lparen S\right\rparen}\cap\mathord{D\left\lparen A\right\rparen}

and the restriction of
L
to the core can be decomposed as
L|_{D}=S-A
.

(D5)

Projection:
Let
P\colon\,H\to H
be an orthogonal projection such that

P(H)\subseteq\mathord{D\left\lparen S\right\rparen}
and
SP=0
as well as

P(D)\subseteq\mathord{D\left\lparen A\right\rparen}
and
AP(D)\subseteq\mathord{D\left\lparen A\right\rparen}
.
Define

P_{S}\colon\ H\mathrel{\longrightarrow}H,\ f\longmapsto Pf+\left\lparen f\mathbin{,}1\right\rparen_{H}.

(D6)

Invariant measure:
Let
\mu
be invariant for
(L,D)
in the sense that

\left\lparen Lf\mathbin{,}1\right\rparen_{H}=\int_{E}Lf\ \mathrm{d}\mu=0\qquad\text{for all }f\in D.

(D7)

semigroup conservativity:

Let
1\in\mathord{D\left\lparen L\right\rparen}
and
L1=0
.

∎

Condition 2.4

(Hypocoercivity conditions
2.4
)
.

(H1)

algebraic relation:

PAP|_{D}=0

(H2)

microscopic coercivity:

\exists\,\Lambda_{m}\in(0,\infty)\,\forall\,f\in D\colon\ \Lambda_{m}\|(\mathord{\mathrm{Id}}-P_{S})f\|_{H}^{2}\leq-\left\lparen Sf\mathbin{,}f\right\rparen_{H}

(H3)

macroscopic coercivity:

\exists\,\Lambda_{M}\in(0,\infty)\,\forall\,f\in\mathord{D\left\lparen\mathchoice{\displaystyle(AP)^{\ast}}{\textstyle(AP)^{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\ast$}}}{\scriptstyle(AP)^{\ast}}{\scriptscriptstyle(AP)^{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\ast$}}}(AP)\right\rparen}\colon\ \Lambda_{M}\|Pf\|_{H}^{2}\leq\|APf\|_{H}^{2}

(H4)

boundedness of auxiliary operators:

\displaystyle\exists\,c_{1},c_{2}\in(0,\infty)\,\forall\,f\in D\colon{}

\displaystyle\|BSf\|_{H}\leq c_{1}\|(\mathord{\mathrm{Id}}-P_{j})f\|_{H}

\displaystyle\land\

\displaystyle\|BA(\mathord{\mathrm{Id}}-P)f\|_{H}\leq c_{2}\|(\mathord{\mathrm{Id}}-P_{j})f\|_{H}

for
B\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=(\mathord{\mathrm{Id}}-(AP)\mathchoice{\displaystyle(AP)^{\ast}}{\textstyle(AP)^{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\ast$}}}{\scriptstyle(AP)^{\ast}}{\scriptscriptstyle(AP)^{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\ast$}}})\mathchoice{\displaystyle(AP)^{\ast}}{\textstyle(AP)^{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\ast$}}}{\scriptstyle(AP)^{\ast}}{\scriptscriptstyle(AP)^{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\ast$}}}
on
D(\mathchoice{\displaystyle(AP)^{\ast}}{\textstyle(AP)^{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\ast$}}}{\scriptstyle(AP)^{\ast}}{\scriptscriptstyle(AP)^{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\ast$}}})

and projections
P_{j}\in\{P,P_{S}\}
,
j\in\{1,2\}
.

∎

2.2.
Bundle measures and weighted bundles

We start with defining bundle measures:
Thinking of measures on some manifold
\mathord{\mathbb{B}}
we should search for induced measures in bundles over
\mathord{\mathbb{B}}
,
i. e. natural measures on the respective total spaces.
Proceeding on the measure theoretic path in the direction of Radon-Nikodým derivatives,
the well-known notion of weighted manifolds is revisited.
The concept of multiplying a density weight function to a volume measure
is not to be confused with conformal transformations of manifolds.
Indeed, conformal transformations affect the geometry;
the difference is the definition of the divergence and
thus the integration by parts formula (
2.1
) below.
As far as we know,
there is very few literature on measure theory paired with the more geometric concept of fibre bundles.
After developing a notion of bundle measures on our own,
we found the seemingly unknown paper
[
Goe59
]
,
where the author concedes more degrees of topological freedom.
Also, there is
[
Nic96
, Section 3.4.5]

wherein the author describes a ‘fibred calculus’ just for smoothly indexed families of manifolds.
We give these references for sake of completeness,
even though we stay with our bespoke construction of bundle measures.

Throughout this section,
let
\mathord{\pi}\colon\,\mathord{\mathbb{E}}\mathrel{\longrightarrow}\mathord{\mathbb{B}}
be a smooth fibre bundle
with standard fibre
F

and define
\mathord{\mathbb{E}}_{b}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathord{\pi}^{-1}(\{b\})
for all
b\in\mathord{\mathbb{B}}
.
Furthermore, we suppose that
the fibre is actually a measurable space
(F,\mathfrak{F})
.
The property of local trivialisation yields a natural
\sigma
-
algebra

\mathop{\mathfrak{B}(\mathord{\mathbb{B}})}\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}\mathfrak{F}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\sigma(\mathfrak{G})
on
\mathord{\mathbb{E}}
with generator

\mathfrak{G}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\left\{\varphi^{-1}(U\times U_{F})\ \middle|\ \begin{matrix}b\in\mathord{\mathbb{B}},\ U_{F}\in\mathfrak{F},\ W\text{ chart domain at\penalty\ }b,\\
U_{b}\subseteq\mathord{\mathbb{B}}\text{ open neighbourhood of\penalty\ }b\text{ s.\,t.}\\
\text{diffeomorphism }\varphi\text{ renders the diagram}\\
\text{in\penalty\ \autoref{fig:local-trivialisation} commutative},\\
U\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=U_{b}\cap W.\end{matrix}\right\}.

One might say that
\mathop{\mathfrak{B}(\mathord{\mathbb{B}})}\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}\mathfrak{F}
is the canonical
\sigma
-
algebra
on
\mathord{\mathbb{E}}
,
however we will refer to it as the
local product
-
\sigma
-
algebra

.
If
\mathfrak{F}=\mathop{\mathfrak{B}(F)}
, then

\mathop{\mathfrak{B}(\mathord{\mathbb{B}})}\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}\mathfrak{F}=\mathop{\mathfrak{B}(\mathord{\mathbb{E}})}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\pi}^{-1}(U_{b})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{b}\times F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathord{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\proj_{1}}

Figure 1.
Local trivialisation of a fibre bundle

In the next definition,
we restrict ourselves to probability measures
as this is the sole situation of interest in the present paper.

Definition 2.5

(bundle measure)
.

Let
\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}
and
\nu_{F}
be probability measures
on
(\mathord{\mathbb{B}},\mathop{\mathfrak{B}(\mathord{\mathbb{B}})})
and
(F,\mathfrak{F})
respectively.
The
(fibre) bundle measure on the total space
\mathord{\mathbb{E}}

is the pullback measure

\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathord{\pi}^{\ast}\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}

of
\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}
wrt.
\mathord{\pi}
supplemented with the fibre measure
\nu_{F}
.
That is the unique probability measure on
\mathord{\mathbb{E}}
satisfying

\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}f\ \mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}=\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\int_{\mathord{\pi}^{-1}(\{b\})}f|_{\mathord{\pi}^{-1}(\{b\})}(e)\ \mathrm{d}\nu_{F}(e)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}(b)

for all bounded
\mathop{\mathfrak{B}(\mathord{\mathbb{B}})}\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}\mathfrak{F}
-
\mathop{\mathfrak{B}(\mathbb{R})}
-
measurable

functions
f\colon\,\mathord{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{R}
.
∎

Remark 2.6
.

In
Definition
2.5
,
the measure
\nu_{F}
on the fibre
\mathord{\pi}^{-1}(\{b\})
is thought as an independent copy of
\nu_{F}
defined on
F
.
By definition a bundle measure yields a disintegration,
but obviously the former concept is motivated by the local product structure of fibre bundles
and tries to find an analogue of product measures respecting this structure,
whereas the latter is kind of the ‘factorisation’ of measures
that are not necessarily product measures.
In all generality,
it’s a difficult problem to give conditions for a disintegration to exists;
in our situation it appears as a byproduct.
∎

The following lemma is stated as an equivalent characterisation of bundle measures,
however it could be formulated as an existence statement, since its proof is constructive.

Lemma 2.7

(bundle measures locally are product measures)
.

Let
\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}
and
\nu_{F}
be probability measures on
\mathord{\mathbb{B}}
and
F
respectively.
Denote by
\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathord{\pi}^{\ast}\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}
the (fibre) bundle measure on
\mathord{\mathbb{E}}
supplemented with fibre measure
\nu_{F}
.
Then,
\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}
is the unique measure on
\mathord{\mathbb{E}}
which locally trivialises to the product measure
\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathbin{\otimes}\nu_{F}
in the following sense:
By definition of fibre bundles,
for any
b\in\mathord{\mathbb{B}}
there is a neighbourhood
U_{b}\subseteq\mathord{\mathbb{B}}
of
b

as well as a diffeomorphism
\varphi

which renders the diagram in
Figure 1
commutative.
Let
V\in\mathop{\mathfrak{B}(\mathord{\mathbb{B}})}\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}\mathfrak{F}
,
W
a chart domain at
b

and define
U\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=W\cap U_{b}
as well as
V_{U}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=V\cap\mathord{\pi}^{-1}(U)
.
Then,
\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}
obeys the transformation rule

\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}=\left\lparen\varphi^{-1}\right\rparen_{\ast}\left\lparen\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathbin{\otimes}\nu_{F}\right\rparen\qquad\text{on }V_{U}.

We say that
\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}
is a loc
-
product measure and
introduce
\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}=\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}\nu_{F}
as the corresponding notation.

Proof.

First, we construct the bundle measure just from the local transformation rule –
basically, that’s the proof of existence of such a measure.
Note that measurable sets of the same form as
V_{U}

generate the local product
-
\sigma
-
algebra
on
\mathord{\mathbb{E}}
,
that they are linked to cylinder sets of the product
-
\sigma
-
algebra
on
U\times F
via
\varphi
,
and that they form a family stable wrt. intersections,
when the empty set is included of course.
Since
\varphi
is continuously invertible, its inverse is measurable.
As the pushforward measure of
\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}
wrt.
\varphi
should be
\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathbin{\otimes}\nu_{F}
,
we declare
\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}
to be
the pushforward measure of
\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathbin{\otimes}\nu_{F}
wrt.
\varphi^{-1}
.
It satisfies the desired transformation rule by construction.
By Fubini-Tonelli this is the particular instance of the integral equation

\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}f\ \mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}=\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\int_{\mathord{\pi}^{-1}(\{b\})}f|_{\mathord{\pi}^{-1}(\{b\})}(e)\ \mathrm{d}\nu_{F}(e)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}(b)

with
f=\mathord{\mathbbm{1}_{V_{U}}}
.
We use this to obtain the general equation via
approximating bounded measurable functions with simple functions and
apply Lebesgue dominated convergence.
The uniqueness assertion is fulfilled by the general uniqueness theorem for measures
which finishes the proof.
∎

Example 2.8

(Möbius strip)
.

A Möbius strip can be thought as fibre bundle
with base manifold
\mathord{\mathbb{B}}=\mathord{\mathbb{S}^{1}}

and standard fibre
F
being an open interval of finite length.
The natural volume measure on the strip coincides on
\mathfrak{G}

with a bundle measure
where the base measure
\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{S}^{1}}}
is the volume measure on the circle
and the fibre measure is the Lebesgue measure on an interval:
\nu_{F}=\mathord{\lambda}
.
Since
\mathfrak{G}\cup\{\emptyset\}
is a generator stable wrt. intersections,
the volume measure on the Möbius strip coincides with the Lebesgue
-
type bundle measure.
For a set
V_{U}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=V\cap\mathord{\pi}^{-1}(U)
we have that

\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}(V_{U})=\int_{\mathord{\pi}(V_{U})\times\proj_{2}\mathbin{\circ}\varphi(V_{U})}\left\lvert\det\left\lparen d\varphi^{-1}\right\rparen\right\rvert\ \mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathbin{\otimes}\nu_{F},

where
\proj_{2}
denotes projection to the second component.
∎

Remark 2.9
.

At this point, it should be clear that

\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{E}};\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}\nu_{F}\right\rparen}

is not isomorphic to the Hilbert space tensor product

\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{B}};\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\right\rparen}\mathbin{\otimes}\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen F;\nu_{F}\right\rparen}
.
Anyway, there might be nice spaces of test functions
that are dense in
\mathord{L^{2}}
-
spaces for bundle measures.
For sake of simplicity,
suppose that the base measure is absolutely continuous wrt. a given Riemannian volume measure
and moreover that

F\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{\text{d}}
is a smooth submanifold,

\mathfrak{F}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=F\cap\mathop{\mathfrak{B}(\mathbb{R}^{\text{d}})}

and
\nu_{F}
is absolutely continuous wrt. restricted Lebesgue measure.
Let
\pi_{0}^{-1}(U)=\varphi^{-1}(U\times F)\in\mathfrak{G}
.
By
[
RS80
, Theorem II.10]

we know that

\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen U\right\rparen}\mathbin{\otimes}\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen F\right\rparen}

is dense in

\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen U\times F;\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathbin{\otimes}\nu_{F}\right\rparen}
,
hence

\displaystyle\varphi_{\ast}\left\lparen\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen U\right\rparen}\mathbin{\otimes}\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen F\right\rparen}\right\rparen

\displaystyle=\varphi_{\ast}\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen U\right\rparen}\mathbin{\otimes}\varphi_{\ast}\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen F\right\rparen}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\linhull\left\{(f\mathbin{\cdot}g)\mathbin{\circ}\varphi\ \middle|\ f\in\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen U\right\rparen},g\in\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen F\right\rparen}\right\}

is dense in

\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\pi_{0}^{-1}(U);\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}\nu_{F}\right\rparen}
.
From a partition of unity argument we can infer
that
\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{E}}\right\rparen}
is dense in

\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{E}};\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}\nu_{F}\right\rparen}
.
∎

Definition 2.10

(weighted (fibre) bundles)
.

Consider functions

\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\colon\,\mathord{\mathbb{B}}\to[0,\infty)

and
\rho_{F}\colon\,F\to[0,\infty)
.
A function
\rho\colon\,\mathord{\mathbb{E}}\to[0,\infty)
is called

bundle weighting with base weight
\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}
and fibre weight
\rho_{F}

if it locally trivialises to the product function

\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathbin{\cdot}\rho_{F}

in the following sense:
Consider the local trivialisation over an open neighbourhood
U_{b}\subseteq\mathord{\mathbb{B}}
at
b

with diffeomorphism
\varphi
rendering the diagram in
Figure 1
commutative.
Then,
\rho
satisfies

\rho(v)=\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}(\mathord{\pi}(v))\mathbin{\cdot}\rho_{F}(\proj_{2}\mathbin{\circ}\varphi(v))\qquad\text{for all }v\in\mathord{\pi}^{-1}(U_{b}).

A fibre bundle together with a bundle weight is called a
weighted (fibre) bundle
.
∎

Lemma 2.11

(existence and uniqueness of bundle weightings)
.

In the situation of
Definition
2.10

a bundle weight
\rho
exists
and is uniquely determined by base weight and fibre weight.
Therefore, we introduce the corresponding notation

\rho=\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}\rho_{F}
.

Proof.

In the local trivialisation
the only possible weight function is given via

\rho=((\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathbin{\circ}\proj_{1})\mathbin{\cdot}(\rho_{F}\mathbin{\circ}\proj_{2}))\mathbin{\circ}\varphi\qquad\text{on }\mathord{\pi}^{-1}(U_{b}).

Fix an open cover of
\mathord{\mathbb{B}}
by chart domains,
then the preimages form an open cover of
\mathord{\mathbb{E}}
.
With a partition of unity subordinate to the latter cover,
we can glue the definitions of
\rho
in the respective local trivialisations together
which finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 2.12

(local Radon-Nikodým derivatives)
.

Suppose some bundle measure
\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}=\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}\nu_{F}
such that

\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}
has a Radon-Nikodým derivative

\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\frac{d\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}}{dm_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}}

wrt. another measure
m_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}
on
(\mathord{\mathbb{B}},\mathop{\mathfrak{B}(\mathord{\mathbb{B}})})

and also
\nu_{F}
has a Radon-Nikodým derivative

\rho_{F}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\frac{d\nu_{F}}{dm_{F}}

wrt. another measure
m_{F}
on
(F,\mathfrak{F})
.
Then, we think of the induced bundle weighting

\rho=\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}\rho_{F}

as a local Radon-Nikodým derivative
in view of it holds

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}=\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}\nu_{F}

\displaystyle=(\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}m_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}})\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}(\rho_{F}m_{F})

\displaystyle=(\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}\rho_{F})(m_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}m_{F})=\rho(m_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}m_{F}).

For sake of brev’ty, we call
\rho

loc
-
density of
\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}
.
∎

Remark 2.13

(Ehresmann connection and weighting)
.

We emphasise again that weighting does not effect geometry:
The Ehresmann connection induced by the metric
\mathord{\mathrm{m}}
on
\mathord{\mathbb{M}}
,
see
Section 2.3
below,
does not change in the course of the weighting procedure.
Indeed, the corresponding Levi-Civita connection
\mathord{\nabla^{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}

is not affected by reweighting as one can easily check
using the Leibniz rule when checking the metric compatibility condition.
Also several geometric objects associated to this connection
just depend on the original metric
\mathord{\mathrm{m}}
and not on the weight function.
∎

Example 2.14

(trivial bundles)
.

If the fibre bundle is trivial,
then bundle measures are pushforwards of product measures
wrt. the trivialisation isomorphism.
Loc
-
densities basically are products of the densities for the respective measures.

One particularly simple example arises with the standard fibre being a singleton.
Then, every fibre measure is absolutely continuous wrt. the Dirac measure for the single point in
F

and fibre weightings reduce to multiplication with a constant factor.
∎

Example 2.15

(weighted manifolds)
.

Consider an orientable Riemannian manifold
(\mathord{\mathbb{B}},\mathord{\mathrm{b}})

and a strictly positive, nonconstant, smooth
3
3

3

Smoothness is actually not required,
we could use e. g. a loc
-
Lipschitzian weight function instead.
But in order to have
Equation (
2.1
)

we need that at least locally a weak gradient of
\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}
exists
and a weak version of Stokes Theorem.

weight function
\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\in\mathord{C^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{B}}\right\rparen}
.
The
Riemannian metric weighted by
\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}

or just
\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}
-
weighted metric
on
\mathord{\mathbb{B}}
is given as

\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}\left\lparen v\mathbin{,}w\right\rparen}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathord{\mathrm{b}\left\lparen\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}v\mathbin{,}\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}w\right\rparen}

for all
v,w\in\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}
with
\pi_{0}(v)=\pi_{0}(w)
.
We do not keep the usual definition of gradients for this weighted metric,
but in fact choose the
\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}
-
weighted gradient
as

\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}}}}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\frac{1}{\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}}\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}
.
I. e. for all smooth vector fields
\mathcal{X}
on
\mathord{\mathbb{B}}
and functions
f\in\mathord{C^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{B}}\right\rparen}
holds

\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}\left\lparen\mathcal{X}\mathbin{,}\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}}}}f\right\rparen}=\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathbin{\cdot}\partial_{\mathcal{X}}f
.
The
\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}
-
weighted divergence
is to be defined as

\mathord{\mathrm{div}_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}}}}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\frac{1}{\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}}\,\mathord{\mathrm{div}_{\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}(\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}^{2}\mathbin{\cdot}\mathord{\mathrm{Id}}),

since the
\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}
-
weighted Laplace-Beltrami operator

\mathop{\Delta_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}}}}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathord{\mathrm{div}_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}}}}(\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}}}})=\frac{1}{\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}}\,\mathord{\mathrm{div}_{\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}(\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}})

shall obey the following integration by parts formula:

(2.1)

\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathop{\Delta_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}}}}f\mathbin{\cdot}g\ \mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}}}}=-\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}}}}f\mathbin{,}\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}}}}g\right\rparen}\ \mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}}}}=-\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathord{\mathrm{b}\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}f\mathbin{,}\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}g\right\rparen}\ \mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}}}}=\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}f\mathbin{\cdot}\mathop{\Delta_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}}}}g\ \mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}}}}

for all
f,g\in\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\right\rparen}
,
where
\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}
and
\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}}}}=\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}

refer to Riemannian volume measures.
If the weight function is constant,
then the definition of the gradient is not to be changed:

\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}}}}=\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}
.
∎

Notation 2.16
.

For sake of readability,
we write
\mathord{L^{p}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{B}};\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}}\right\rparen}

instead of
\mathord{L^{p}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{B}};\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}}}}\right\rparen}
.
Similar notation is used for other spaces depending on a weighted Riemannian volume measure.
∎

Remark 2.17

(adjoint vector fields wrt. weighted metric)
.

Let
(\mathord{\mathbb{B}},\mathord{\mathrm{b}})
be an orientable Riemannian manifold.
The general form of an adjoint vector field is given via the Divergence Theorem:
If
\mathcal{X}
is a vector field on
\mathord{\mathbb{B}}
,
then its adjoint wrt. the Riemannian metric,
i e. wrt. the
\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{B}};\mathord{\mathrm{b}}\right\rparen}
-
scalar product,
is
\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathcal{X}^{\ast}}{\textstyle\mathcal{X}^{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\ast$}}}{\scriptstyle\mathcal{X}^{\ast}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{X}^{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\ast$}}}=-\mathcal{X}-\mathord{\mathrm{div}}_{\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}(\mathcal{X})
.
Thus, solenoidal vector fields could be viewed as antisymmetric operators.
Introducing a smooth, nonconstant weight function
\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}
on
\mathord{\mathbb{B}}
as above yields

\displaystyle\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathcal{X}f\mathbin{\cdot}g\ \mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}}}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}-f\mathcal{X}(\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}g)-\mathord{\mathrm{div}_{\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}(\mathcal{X})\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}fg\ \mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}-\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}f\mathcal{X}g-fg\mathcal{X}\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}-\mathord{\mathrm{div}_{\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}(\mathcal{X})\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}fg\ \mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}-f\mathcal{X}g-fg\,\frac{1}{\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}}\,\mathcal{X}\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}-\mathord{\mathrm{div}_{\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}(\mathcal{X})fg\ \mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}}}}

for all
f,g\in\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{B}}\right\rparen}
.
Hence, the adjoint of
\mathcal{X}
wrt.
\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}}
reads as

\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathcal{X}^{\ast}}{\textstyle\mathcal{X}^{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\ast$}}}{\scriptstyle\mathcal{X}^{\ast}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{X}^{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\ast$}}}=-\mathcal{X}-\mathord{\mathrm{div}}_{\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}(\mathcal{X})-\frac{1}{\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}}\mathcal{X}\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}.

Therefore, we would correct the differential operator
\mathcal{X}
for a vector field
\mathcal{X}
solenoidal wrt.
\mathord{\mathrm{b}}
by the
logarithmic derivative of
\rho
along
\mathcal{X}

to become antisymmetric again,
i. e. replace
\mathcal{X}
by
\mathcal{X}+\frac{1}{\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}}\mathcal{X}\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}
.
∎

Lemma 2.18

(weighted Laplace-Beltrami in terms of logarithmic derivative)
.

Again, let
(\mathord{\mathbb{B}},\mathord{\mathrm{b}})
be an orientable Riemannian manifold
weighted by
\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}
strictly positive, nonconstant and smooth.
The weighted Laplace-Beltrami is written as

\mathop{\Delta_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}}}}=\mathop{\Delta_{\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}+\frac{1}{\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}}\,\mathord{\mathrm{b}\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathbin{,}\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}\right\rparen}=\mathop{\Delta_{\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}+\frac{1}{\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}}\,\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}.

We call the second summand the
logarithmic derivative of
\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}
.
In particular, for
\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}=\exp(-\psi)
with
\psi\in\mathord{C^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{B}}\right\rparen}

we obtain

\mathop{\Delta_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{b}}}}}=\mathop{\Delta_{\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}-\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}\psi.

Proof.

Substantially, the proof looks like in the Euclidean case
as we need just the Leibniz rule, Stokes Theorem and the defining characterisation of the gradient.
∎

Later on, we will have to talk about Poincaré inequalities.
For this purpose among others,
we shall fix some notions concerning sections in general.
In a nutshell, we are relying on the assumption that
smooth vector fields viewed as first order differential operators
could equivalently seen as smooth sections.
This is true in all applications we are interested in,
but we are aware of counterexamples like noncommutative tori,
see
[
Ros13
]
.
In this paper, we just leave issues of noncommutative geometry aside
and won’t mention them again.

Notation 2.19

(space of sections)
.

We denote by
\mathord{\Gamma\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{B}};\mathord{\mathbb{E}}\right\rparen}
the space of measurable sections.
If no confusion is possible,
we omit the base manifold writing just
\mathord{\Gamma\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{E}}\right\rparen}
.
Moreover, we denote the space of
m
-times continuously differentiable sections
by
\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\Gamma^{m}}{\textstyle\Gamma^{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}}}{\scriptstyle\Gamma^{m}}{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma^{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}}}(\mathord{\mathbb{B}};\mathord{\mathbb{E}})}
or just
\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\Gamma^{m}}{\textstyle\Gamma^{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}}}{\scriptstyle\Gamma^{m}}{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma^{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m$}}}(\mathord{\mathbb{E}})}
.
As usual the differentiability parameter equals the regularity of the differentiable structure,
thus it is
m=\infty
in our context for sake of simplicity.
∎

For the rest of this subsection,
we consider
F
to be a Banach space, i. e. the fibre bundle is a Banach bundle,
see e. g.
[
Lan95
, Chapter III]
.
This gives us a section
\lvert\cdot\rvert_{\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}

in the bundle of functions
\mathord{\mathbb{E}}\to\mathbb{R}
over
\mathord{\mathbb{B}}
such that

\lvert\cdot\rvert_{\mathord{\mathbb{E}},b}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\lvert\cdot\rvert_{\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}(b)\colon\,\mathord{\mathbb{E}}_{b}\to\mathbb{R}

is a norm
and
(\mathord{\mathbb{E}}_{b},\lvert\cdot\rvert_{\mathord{\mathbb{E}},b})
is a Banach space
for all
b\in\mathord{\mathbb{B}}
.
One might think of
\lvert\cdot\rvert_{\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}
as a ‘Riemannian norm’,
but should be careful since it is not necessarily related to a Riemannian metric.
Furthermore, we assume a
\sigma
-
finite measure
\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}
on
(\mathord{\mathbb{B}},\mathop{\mathfrak{B}(\mathord{\mathbb{B}})})
.

Definition 2.20

(Integrable sections)
.

Let
p\in[1,\infty)
and
\mathcal{X}\in\mathord{\Gamma\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{E}}\right\rparen}
.
We call
\mathcal{X}

p
-
integrable wrt.
\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}
,
if the integral

\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\lvert\mathcal{X}(b)\rvert_{\mathord{\mathbb{E}},b}^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}(b)

is finite.
By
\mathord{L^{p}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{B}}\to\mathord{\mathbb{E}};\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\right\rparen}

we denote the set of equivalence classes of
p
-
integrable sections
wrt. equality
\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}
-
almost everywhere.
Clearly, we endow
\mathord{L^{p}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{B}}\to\mathord{\mathbb{E}};\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\right\rparen}
with the norm

\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathord{L^{p}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{B}}\to\mathord{\mathbb{E}};\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\right\rparen}}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\left\lparen\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\lvert\cdot\rvert_{\mathord{\mathbb{E}},b}^{p}\ \mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}(b)\right\rparen^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{p}}}.

As usual for the case
p=\infty
, we define
\mathord{L^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{B}}\to\mathord{\mathbb{E}};\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\right\rparen}

as the space of measurable sections
\mathcal{X}\in\mathord{\Gamma\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{E}}\right\rparen}
which are bounded almost everywhere, i. e. there is a
c\in(0,\infty)
such that

\lvert\mathcal{X}(b)\rvert_{\mathord{\mathbb{E}},b}\leq c

for
\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}
-
almost all
b\in\mathord{\mathbb{B}}
.
The norm of such a
\mathcal{X}\in\mathord{L^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{B}}\to\mathord{\mathbb{E}};\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\right\rparen}

is the infimum of all such bounds
c
.
∎

Completeness of these spaces of integrable sections is shown
like in the Fischer-Riesz Theorem for usual
\mathord{L^{p}}
-
spaces.
One might say that we defined
\mathord{L^{p}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{B}}\to\mathord{\mathbb{E}};\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\right\rparen}

as a ‘direct integral of Banach spaces’.
This is merely a verbalisation of a more general concept of
integrating homoousios fibres wrt. some (
\sigma
-
finite) measure on the base space.
This belongs to mathematical folklore and
we can not discuss this construction here in exhausting detail.

Example 2.21

(direct integral of Hilbert space fibres)
.

Let
\mathord{\mathbb{B}}
be a
b
-
dimensional Riemannian manifold,
the standard fibre
F
be
\mathbb{R}^{\text{d}}
for some natural number
d

and let a section
\mathord{\mathrm{b}}
in the bundle of symmetric bilinear forms

\mathrm{Sym}(\mathord{\mathbb{E}})\rightarrow\mathord{\mathbb{B}}

such that it is pointwise positive semidefinite.
Then,
\mathord{\mathrm{b}}
induces a section of norms via

\lvert e\rvert_{\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathord{\mathrm{b}_{\mathord{\pi}(e)}\left\lparen e\mathbin{,}e\right\rparen}^{\frac{1}{2}}\qquad\text{for all }e\in\mathord{\mathbb{E}}

and all fibres are Hilbert spaces,
since all norms on
\mathbb{R}^{\text{d}}
are equivalent.
Thus, we can describe the space of square-integrable sections
as the direct integral

\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}^{\oplus}\mathord{\mathbb{E}}_{b}\ \mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}(b)

wrt.
\mu_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}
.
This gives us a Hilbert space again.
Of course,
(\mathord{\mathbb{B}},\mathord{\mathrm{b}})
being a Riemannian manifold
would be the most interesting case, i. e.
\mathord{\mathbb{E}}=\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}
with
F=\mathbb{R}^{\text{b}}

and the section
\mathord{\mathrm{b}}
being a Riemannian metric.
Then, embracing
Notation
2.16
we have that

\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{B}}\to\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{B}}};\mathord{\mathrm{b}}\right\rparen}=\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}^{\oplus}\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{b}}{\textstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle b$}}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{b}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle b$}}}\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\ \mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}(b).

∎

In principle, a function
u\in\mathord{L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{B}};\mathord{\mathrm{b}}\right\rparen}
on a Riemannian manifold
(\mathord{\mathbb{B}},\mathord{\mathrm{b}})

is weakly differentiable
if the composition
u\mathbin{\circ}b
is weakly differentiable for every chart
b
.
For the more formal (topological) definition of Sobolev spaces
\mathord{H^{m,p}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{B}}\right\rparen}
on
\mathord{\mathbb{B}}
we refer to
[
Heb99
, Section 2.2]
.
As in the case of Sobolev spaces,
we are not content with characterising a weak gradient in charts
as vector field with locally integrable components,
but from the existence proof for Sobolev spaces as function spaces
we know the coordinate-free description of weak gradients:
A function
u
is weakly differentiable
if there is an element

\mathcal{U}\in\mathord{L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{B}}\to\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{B}}};\mathord{\mathrm{b}}\right\rparen}
satisfying

\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathord{\mathrm{b}\left\lparen\mathcal{X}\mathbin{,}\mathcal{U}\right\rparen}\ \mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}=-\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathord{\mathrm{div}_{\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}(\mathcal{X})\mathbin{\cdot}u\ \mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}\qquad\text{for all }\mathcal{X}\in\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\Gamma_{c}^{\infty}}{\textstyle\Gamma_{c}^{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}{\scriptstyle\Gamma_{c}^{\infty}}{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma_{c}^{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}(\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{B}}})}.

We denote such a weak gradient as
\mathcal{U}=\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}u
in analogy to the usual gradient.
In case of
u\in\mathord{H^{1,p}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{B}}\right\rparen}

we have that

\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}u\in\mathord{L^{p}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{B}}\to\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{B}}};\mathord{\mathrm{b}}\right\rparen}
.
The previous characterisation instances to

\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathcal{U}\varphi\ \mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}=\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\mathord{\mathrm{b}\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}\varphi\mathbin{,}\mathcal{U}\right\rparen}\ \mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}=-\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}u\mathop{\Delta_{\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}\varphi\ \mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}\qquad\text{for all }\varphi\in\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{B}}\right\rparen}.

If

\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}u\mathop{\Delta_{\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}=0
for all test functions
\varphi\in\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{B}}\right\rparen}
,
then we call
u

weakly harmonic
.
Using this definition
we bypass the problem to extend the divergence operator –
which is taking the trace of the covariant derivative –
with domain
\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\textstyle\Gamma^{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}{\scriptstyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma^{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\right\rparen}
to all the possible weak gradients in the Banach space
\mathord{L^{p}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{B}}\to\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\right\rparen}
.
In view of Weyl’s Lemma characterising weakly harmonic functions
those functions can be thought as harmonic functions,
since we don’t have boundaries in this paper.

Example 2.22

(loc-Lipschitz potentials)
.

We know that
if
\psi
is Lipschitzian
4
4

4

We say that functions
f\colon\,\mathord{\mathbb{B}}\to\mathbb{R}
are Lipschitzian if
there is a positive constant
C
such that

|f(x)-f(y)|\leq Cd_{\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}(x,y)

for all
x,y\in\mathord{\mathbb{B}}
,
where we denote by
d_{\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}
the intrinsic metric induced by the Riemannian metric
\mathord{\mathrm{b}}
.
As a consequence,
if we carelessly embed
\mathord{\mathbb{B}}
into some
\mathbb{R}^{\text{d}}
large enough,
the embedding might change the structure of the metric space and
thus the class of Lipschitz functions.

with compact support,
then
\psi\in\mathord{H^{1,p}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{B}}\right\rparen}
for all
p\in[1,\infty]

meaning that
\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}\psi
exists as a weak gradient,
see
[
Heb99
, Proposition 2.4]
.
Indeed, if
\mathord{\mathbb{B}}
is a compact manifold,
then the proposition applies to any Lipschitzian
\psi
;
in general, we have not a compact manifold,
but consider arbitrary compact subsets instead.
∎

2.3.
Ehresmann connections and Sasaki metric

In this subsection,
we introduce Ehresmann connections
as a decomposition of the double tangent bundle in terms of a Whitney sum
as well as the Sasaki metric on the tangent space
as the Riemannian metric respecting the entire Ehresmann connection.

Definition 2.23

(vertical bundle)
.

Let
\mathord{\pi}\colon\,\mathord{\mathbb{E}}\mathrel{\longrightarrow}\mathord{\mathbb{B}}
be a (smooth) fibre bundle.
The
space of vertical (tangent) vectors
is

\mathord{\mathrm{V}\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathrm{Null}\left\lparen d\mathord{\pi}\right\rparen
,
the nullspace of the differential

d\mathord{\pi}\colon\,\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}\to\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}
.
Vertical vectors are thought as being tangent to the fibres of
\mathord{\pi}
.
This yields the so-called
vertical bundle

\pi_{0}\colon\,\mathord{\mathrm{V}\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}\mathrel{\longrightarrow}\mathord{\mathbb{E}}
.
As
d_{v}\mathord{\pi}
is surjective for all
v\in\mathord{\mathbb{E}}
,
the vertical bundle is a smooth subbundle.
Smooth sections in this bundle are called
vertical
.

Additionally, if
\mathord{\pi}\colon\,\mathord{\mathbb{E}}\mathrel{\longrightarrow}\mathord{\mathbb{B}}
happens to be a vector bundle,
than we can define the
vertical lift at
v

\vertlift_{v}\colon\ \mathord{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathord{\pi}(v)}\to\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{v}}{\textstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle v$}}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle v$}}}\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}

for
v\in\mathord{\mathbb{E}}
fixed
via the action on arbitrary test functions
f\in\mathord{C^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathord{\pi}(v)}\right\rparen}
as

\mathord{\left\langle\vertlift_{v}(w)\mathbin{,}d_{v}f\right\rangle}=\frac{d}{dt}f(v+tw)\bigr|_{t=0}
.
If
\mathord{\mathbb{E}}=\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}
, then

\mathord{\left\langle\vertlift_{v}(w)\mathbin{,}d_{v}(df)\right\rangle}=\mathord{\left\langle w\mathbin{,}df\right\rangle}\qquad\text{for all }f\in\mathord{C^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{B}}\right\rparen}

determines the lift uniquely.
The smooth section

\mathcal{V}\in\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\textstyle\Gamma^{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}{\scriptstyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma^{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{E}};\mathord{\mathrm{V}\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}\right\rparen}
given as

v\mapsto\mathcal{V}(v)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\vertlift_{v}(v)

is called
canonical vector field
.
Furthermore,
the
vertical projection
\mathord{\mathrm{vpr}}

is given at
v\in\mathord{\mathbb{E}}
as
the projection mapping
\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{v}}{\textstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle v$}}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle v$}}}\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}\to\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{V}_{v}}{\textstyle\mathrm{V}_{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle v$}}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{V}_{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{V}_{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle v$}}}\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}
.
∎

Now, we restrict ourselves to the case of vector bundles, but
a definition of Ehresmann connections for fibre bundles can be found
e. g. in
[
KMS93
, Section 9]
.

Definition 2.24

(Ehresmann connection)
.

Let
\mathord{\pi}\colon\,\mathord{\mathbb{E}}\mathrel{\longrightarrow}\mathord{\mathbb{B}}
be a (smooth) vector bundle.
A (smooth) subbundle
\mathord{\mathrm{H}\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}\leq\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}

is called
Ehresmann connection
or
horizontal (tangent) bundle
if

\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{v}}{\textstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle v$}}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle v$}}}\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}=\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{V}_{v}}{\textstyle\mathrm{V}_{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle v$}}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{V}_{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{V}_{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle v$}}}\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}\oplus\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{v}}{\textstyle\mathrm{H}_{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle v$}}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{H}_{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}_{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle v$}}}\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}\qquad\text{for all }v\in\mathord{\mathbb{E}}.

For sake of readability we just write

\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}=\mathord{\mathrm{V}\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}\oplus\mathord{\mathrm{H}\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}

in the sense of a Whitney sum.

The
horizontal lift at
v
of
w\in\mathord{\mathbb{E}}
is the unique vector
\horlift_{v}(w)\in\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{H}_{v}}{\textstyle\mathrm{H}_{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle v$}}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{H}_{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{H}_{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle v$}}}\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}
such that

w=\mathord{\left\langle\horlift_{v}(w)\mathbin{,}d\mathord{\pi}\right\rangle}.

Finally, the projection of tangent vectors to their horizontal parts is denoted by
\mathord{\mathrm{hpr}}
.
∎

Compare this usage of the terms ‘vertical’ and ‘horizontal’
to the usage in stochastic analysis as e. g. in
[
WI81
, Section V.4]
or
[
Hsu02
, Chapter 2]
.
Luckily, we escaped the frame bundle via the McKean-Gangolli injection scheme
as described by E. Jøergensen.

Henceforth, we consider
\mathord{\mathbb{B}}=\mathord{\mathbb{M}}
with the properties
2.1
,
since this is the sole instance of interest for this paper.
Furthermore, we just take the tangent space
\mathord{\mathbb{E}}=\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}
,
but note that we are interested in
\mathord{\mathrm{S}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\subseteq\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}

in case of the fibre lay-down model.
Moreover, let us specify the one Ehresmann connection
we always will consider without further mentioning:
the Riemannian horizontal bundle.

Definition 2.25

(connector map and Riemannian horizontal bundle)
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathord{\mathbb{M}}
be a neighbourhood of
o\in\mathord{\mathbb{M}}

with preimage
V\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\pi_{0}^{-1}(U)\subseteq\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}
such that
the exponential
\exp_{o}\colon\,\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{o}}{\textstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle o$}}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{o}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle o$}}}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\to\mathord{\mathbb{M}}

maps a 0
-
neighbourhood to
V
diffeomorphically.
Let
\tau\colon\,V\to\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{o}}{\textstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle o$}}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{o}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle o$}}}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}
denote parallel transport of
v\in V
along the unique geodesic arc connecting
\pi(v)
and
o
.
Let
r_{-u}\colon\,\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{o}}{\textstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle o$}}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{o}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle o$}}}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\to\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{o}}{\textstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle o$}}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{o}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle o$}}}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}

be the translation
w\mapsto w-u
by the vector
u\in\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{o}}{\textstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle o$}}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{o}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle o$}}}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}
.
Now, consider the mapping

\kappa\colon\ V\mathrel{\longrightarrow}\mathord{\mathbb{M}},\ v\longmapsto\left\lparen\exp_{o}\mathbin{\circ}r_{-u}\mathbin{\circ}\tau\right\rparen(v).

The dependency on the chart vanishes when passing to the differential

d_{u}\kappa\colon\ \mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{u}}{\textstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{u}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}}\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\mathrel{\longrightarrow}\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{\pi_{0}(u)}}{\textstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\pi_{0}(u)$}}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{\pi_{0}(u)}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\pi_{0}(u)$}}}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}},\ a\longmapsto\mathord{\left\langle a\mathbin{,}d(\exp_{\pi_{0}(u)}\mathbin{\circ}r_{-u}\mathbin{\circ}\tau)\right\rangle}

which is called the
connector map
,
cf.
[
Dom62
, Section 2]
.
Also see
[
Sak96
, Section II.4]
for explanation in terms of local coordinates.
Via the assignment

\mathord{\mathrm{H}\mathord{\mathbb{E}}}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathrm{Null}\left\lparen d\kappa\right\rparen

we gain an Ehresmann connection
which we call the
Riemannian horizontal bundle
,
see e. g
[
Dom62
, Appendix (ii)]
.
As the exponential map depends on the given Riemannian metric,
so does this horizontal bundle.
∎

Some authors call
d\kappa
the vertical projection
and
d\pi_{0}
the horizontal projection.
E. g. in
Notation
2.26

we use the mappings in a way that would justify such a naming.
However, we do not recommend this terminology
and introduced the notions ‘
\mathord{\mathrm{vpr}}
’ and ‘
\mathord{\mathrm{hpr}}
’ to avoid confusion.
Concerning the vertical or horizontal lift of functions
there is a well-established consent what it should be.

Notation 2.26

(vertical/horizontal lift of functions)
.

Let
f_{0}
be a real
-
valued function with domain in
\mathord{\mathbb{M}}
.
We call the pullback of
f_{0}
wrt. the tangent bundle projection
\pi_{0}
the
vertical lift of
f_{0}
.
For sake of brev’ty we define
f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\pi_{0}^{\ast}f_{0}

whenever it’s defined.

A direct analogy would be that
the horizontal lift of
f_{0}
is the pullback wrt. the connector map
and we declare
f_{0}^{\mathrm{h}}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\kappa^{\ast}f_{0}

whenever it’s defined.
However, this is not straight forward,
since
\kappa
depends on the choice of base point
o
of the exponential and the translation vector
u
.
At first, the horizontal lift of a smooth function
f_{0}
is a function

(u,v)\longmapsto\left\lparen f_{0}\mathbin{\circ}\exp_{\pi_{0}(u)}\mathbin{\circ}r_{-u}\mathbin{\circ}\tau\right\rparen(v)

or a bevy of functions indexed by
u
;
we should exclude the case that
u
and
v
are in the same fibre
as this would yield the vertical lift again.
We do not worry too much about well-definedness and differentiability:
Due to assumption
(M2)
and the Hopf-Rinow Theorem
the exponential mapping at
\pi_{0}(u)
is defined everywhere on the tangent space,
furthermore it is almost everywhere a diffeomorphism by
[
Sak96
, Lemma III.4.4]
.
But as aforementioned earlier
we can project from the double tangent space into the tangent space via
d\pi_{0}
and
d\kappa

and get

df_{0}(d\pi_{0}+d\kappa)=df_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}+df_{0}\mathbin{\circ}d\kappa
.
The
horizontal lift of
f_{0}
is a function
f_{0}^{\mathrm{h}}
such that

\mathord{\left\langle a\mathbin{,}df_{0}^{\mathrm{h}}\right\rangle}=\mathord{\left\langle a\mathbin{,}df_{0}\mathbin{\circ}d\kappa\right\rangle}

for all
a\in\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}
–
algebraically this makes perfectly sense and is unique up to constant offsets.
A more analytic intuition would be

f_{0}^{\mathrm{h}}(v)=\int_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{v}}{\textstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle v$}}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle v$}}}\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}df_{0}\mathbin{\circ}d\kappa\,\mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda}
,
where we consider the Lebesgue measure on the standard fibre
F=\mathbb{R}^{2\text{n}}
of the double tangent bundle.
∎

Lemma 2.27
.

Let
f_{0}\in\mathord{C^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\right\rparen}

and
\mathcal{X}\in\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\textstyle\Gamma^{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}{\scriptstyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma^{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\right\rparen}
.
Then, the following relations hold

\displaystyle\vertlift(\mathcal{X})f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}=0\quad

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\horlift(\mathcal{X})f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}=\left\lparen\mathcal{X}f_{0}\right\rparen^{\mathrm{v}},

\displaystyle\vertlift(\mathcal{X})f_{0}^{\mathrm{h}}=\left\lparen\mathcal{X}f_{0}\right\rparen^{\mathrm{v}}\quad

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\horlift(\mathcal{X})f_{0}^{\mathrm{h}}=0.

Proof.

Elementary.
∎

Next, we define the Sasaki metric.
It’s original definition was given in the famous paper
[
Sas58
]

containing many other results of general interest.
Sasaki refered just to the Riemannian horizontal bundle and so do we,
but it’s clear that the following definition can easily be adapted to a situation without connector map.

Definition 2.28

(Sasaki metric)
.

The
Sasaki metric
\mathord{\mathrm{s}}
on
\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}

is uniquely characterised as
the Riemannian metric respecting inner products in the given Ehresmann connection, i. e. the metric
\mathord{\mathrm{s}}
is
natural
in the sense of
[
GK02
]
meaning that

\mathord{\mathrm{s}\left\lparen\horlift(\mathcal{X})\mathbin{,}\horlift(\mathcal{Y})\right\rparen}=\mathord{\mathrm{m}\left\lparen\mathcal{X}\mathbin{,}\mathcal{Y}\right\rparen}\mathbin{\circ}\pi_{0}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathord{\mathrm{s}\left\lparen\horlift(\mathcal{X})\mathbin{,}\vertlift(\mathcal{Y})\right\rparen}=0

for all
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}\in\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\textstyle\Gamma^{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}{\scriptstyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma^{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\right\rparen}
,
and additionally the metric respects the inner product under vertical lifting in the sense that

\mathord{\mathrm{s}\left\lparen\vertlift(\mathcal{X})\mathbin{,}\vertlift(\mathcal{Y})\right\rparen}=\mathord{\mathrm{m}\left\lparen\mathcal{X}\mathbin{,}\mathcal{Y}\right\rparen}\mathbin{\circ}\pi_{0}

for all
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}\in\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\textstyle\Gamma^{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}{\scriptstyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma^{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\right\rparen}
.
This metric is explicitly given as the sum of pullbacks of the metric tensor of the base manifold:

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathrm{s}\left\lparen a\mathbin{,}b\right\rparen}

\displaystyle{}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\kappa^{\ast}\mathord{\mathrm{m}\left\lparen a\mathbin{,}b\right\rparen}+\pi_{0}^{\ast}\mathord{\mathrm{m}\left\lparen a\mathbin{,}b\right\rparen}

\displaystyle=\mathord{\mathrm{m}\left\lparen\mathord{\left\langle a\mathbin{,}d\kappa\right\rangle}\mathbin{,}\mathord{\left\langle b\mathbin{,}d\kappa\right\rangle}\right\rparen}+\mathord{\mathrm{m}\left\lparen\mathord{\left\langle a\mathbin{,}d\pi_{0}\right\rangle}\mathbin{,}\mathord{\left\langle b\mathbin{,}d\pi_{0}\right\rangle}\right\rparen}

for all
a,b\in\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}
.
We call the component
\mathord{\mathrm{h}}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\pi_{0}^{\ast}\mathord{\mathrm{m}}
the
horizontal (Sasaki) metric
.
Mutatis mutandis, the
vertical (Sasaki) metric

\mathord{\mathrm{v}}
is
\mathord{\mathrm{v}}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\kappa^{\ast}\mathord{\mathrm{m}}
.
This yields
\mathord{\mathrm{s}}=\mathord{\mathrm{v}}+\mathord{\mathrm{h}}
.
∎

Notation 2.29
.

If we consider the
\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}
-
weighted manifold
(\mathord{\mathbb{M}},\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}})
,
then the weighting procedure is naturally reflected
in the horizontal bundle via the vertical lift.
Explicitly, we think of
(\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}},\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{h}}})

as a
\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}^{\mathrm{v}}
-
weighted Riemannian manifold,
where
\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{h}}}
is the weighted version of the horizontal Sasaki metric
\mathord{\mathrm{h}}
.
In the first place, the vertical bundle is not affected.
If we weight the fibre
F=\mathbb{R}^{\text{n}}
by
\rho_{F}
,
then the vertical metric changes and the horizontal one does not.
The weighted vertical Sasaki metric is denoted by
\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}
.
If and only if a bundle weighting is specified and no confusion possible,
we denote the corresponding weighted Sasaki metric by
\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}
and the weighted tangent bundle just as
(\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}},\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}})
.
∎

It turns out that the volume form corresponding to the Sasaki metric –
it exists independently of orientability of
\mathord{\mathbb{M}}
as tangent spaces always are orientable –
induces a volume measure
with very neat loc-product structure.

Lemma 2.30

(Sasakian volume measure)
.

The volume measure
\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{s}}}}
wrt. the Sasaki metric
coincides with the bundle measure on
\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}
supplemented with Lebesgue fibre measure,
i. e. it holds

\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{s}}}}=\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}\mathord{\lambda}
,
where we abbreviated the
n
-
fold Lebesgue measure

\mathord{\lambda}^{\otimes\text{n}}
just by
\mathord{\lambda}
.

Proof.

Form
Definition
2.28

we know that the Sasaki metric can be written as the sum

\mathord{\mathrm{s}}=\mathord{\mathrm{v}}+\mathord{\mathrm{h}}

of vertical and horizontal metric.
We encounter this situation when considering a product of Riemannian manifolds and
thus, we know that the Sasakian volume measure is a product measure basically.
Indeed, let a chart
(v^{j})_{j=1}^{2\text{n}}
with domain
V\subseteq\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}

respecting the Ehresmann connection
in the sense that
(\partial v^{j})_{j=1}^{\text{n}}
provides local basis for
either the vertical or the horizontal vector fields and

(\partial v^{j})_{j=\text{n}+1}^{2\text{n}}
provides a basis for the complementary type of vector fields;
the matrix representation of the Sasaki metric is of block diagonal form
with zero matrix at south west and north east position.
Therefore, the Sasakian volume form
\mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{s}}}}
reads in those coordinates as

\mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{s}}}}=\sqrt{\left\lvert\pi_{0}^{\ast}\mathord{\mathrm{m}}\right\rvert}\sqrt{\left\lvert\kappa^{\ast}\mathord{\mathrm{m}}\right\rvert}\bigwedge_{j=1}^{2\text{n}}\mathrm{d}v^{j}.

Wlog.
V
is preimage of a chart domain in
\mathord{\mathbb{M}}
, i. e.
V=\pi_{0}^{-1}(U)
,
such that there is a diffeomorphism
\varphi

rendering the diagram in
Figure 1
commutative.
Hence the pushforward measure
\varphi_{\ast}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{s}}}}
has to coincide
with product volume measure on
U\times F
,
where the fibre
F=\mathbb{R}^{\text{n}}
is naturally endowed with the Lebesgue measure.
But
Lemma
2.7

uniquely determines the bundle measure on
\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}
supplemented the fibre measure
\nu_{F}=\mathord{\lambda}
,
so the Sasakian volume measure has to be this bundle measure.
∎

Note that by
Lemma
2.27

we already know that
the gradients
\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}
and
\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{h}}}}
wrt. vertical and horizontal Sasaki metric respectively
satisfy
\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}=0
and
\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{h}}}}f_{0}^{\mathrm{h}}=0
.
In the next lemma we give more insight into the three basic operators
induced by the metrics from the Ehresmann connection.

Lemma 2.31

(Sasakian gradient, divergence and Laplacian)
.

The gradient, divergence and Laplace-Beltrami operators
corresponding to the vertical Sasaki metric are characterised by

\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}f_{0}^{\mathrm{h}}=\vertlift\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}f_{0}\mathbin{\circ}\pi_{0}\right\rparen,\ \mathord{\mathrm{div}_{\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\left\lparen\vertlift{\mathcal{X}}\right\rparen=\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{div}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}\mathcal{X}\right\rparen\mathbin{\circ}\pi_{0}\text{ and }\mathop{\Delta_{\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}f_{0}^{\mathrm{h}}=\left\lparen\mathop{\Delta_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}f_{0}\right\rparen\mathbin{\circ}\pi_{0}

for all
f_{0}\in\mathord{C^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\right\rparen}

and
