\mathcal{X}\in\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\textstyle\Gamma^{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}{\scriptstyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma^{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\right\rparen}
.
Similarly, for the case of the horizontal Sasaki metric we have that

\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{h}}}}f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}=\horlift\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}f_{0}\mathbin{\circ}\pi_{0}\right\rparen,\ \mathord{\mathrm{div}_{\mathord{\mathrm{h}}}}\left\lparen\horlift{\mathcal{X}}\right\rparen=\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{div}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}\mathcal{X}\right\rparen\mathbin{\circ}\pi_{0}\text{ and }\mathop{\Delta_{\mathord{\mathrm{h}}}}f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}=\left\lparen\mathop{\Delta_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}f_{0}\right\rparen\mathbin{\circ}\pi_{0}

for all
f_{0}\in\mathord{C^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\right\rparen}

and
\mathcal{X}\in\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\textstyle\Gamma^{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}{\scriptstyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma^{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\right\rparen}
.

Eventually, we have

\mathord{\mathrm{div}_{\mathord{\mathrm{s}}}}(\mathcal{Y})=\mathord{\mathrm{div}_{\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}(\mathord{\mathrm{vpr}}(\mathcal{Y}))+\mathord{\mathrm{div}_{\mathord{\mathrm{h}}}}(\mathord{\mathrm{hpr}}(\mathcal{Y}))\qquad\text{for all }\mathcal{Y}\in\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\textstyle\Gamma^{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}{\scriptstyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma^{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}};\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\right\rparen}.

Proof.

We restrict ourselves to the vertical case, since the other statements follow analogously.
Let
f_{0}\in\mathord{C^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\right\rparen}

and
\mathcal{X}\in\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\textstyle\Gamma^{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}{\scriptstyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma^{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\right\rparen}
arbitrary be fixed.
Then, by definition and from
Lemma
2.27

we know that the following two equations hold simultaneously
which characterises the vertical gradient:

\displaystyle\mathord{\mathrm{v}\left\lparen\vertlift\mathcal{X}\mathbin{,}\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}f_{0}^{\mathrm{h}}\right\rparen}

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{X}f_{0})\mathbin{\circ}\pi_{0}

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\mathord{\mathrm{v}\left\lparen\vertlift\mathcal{X}\mathbin{,}\vertlift\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}f_{0}\right\rparen\right\rparen}

\displaystyle=\mathord{\mathrm{m}\left\lparen\mathcal{X}\mathbin{,}\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}f_{0}\right\rparen}=(\mathcal{X}f_{0})\mathbin{\circ}\pi_{0}.

Regarding the divergence,
we use the definition in terms of the Lie derivative,
which is mostly preferred in manifold theory,
and apply Cartan’s magical formula.
We have for the Riemannian metrics
\mathord{\mathrm{m}}
and
\mathord{\mathrm{v}}
the defining equations of divergence:

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{X}}}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}

\displaystyle=\mathord{\mathrm{div}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}(\mathcal{X})\mathbin{\cdot}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\mathop{\mathscr{L}_{\vertlift\mathcal{X}}}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}

\displaystyle=\mathord{\mathrm{div}_{\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}(\vertlift\mathcal{X})\mathbin{\cdot}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}},

where ‘
\mathop{\mathscr{L}}
’ denotes the Lie derivative.
Again, the symbol
\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}
is to be interpreted
as either volume form or 1
-
density.
Now, in case of a volume form Cartan’s formula yields the equation

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathscr{L}_{\vertlift\mathcal{X}}}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}

\displaystyle=d\left\lparen\vertlift(\mathcal{X})\mathrel{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\lnot$}}}\kappa^{\ast}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}\right\rparen+\vertlift(\mathcal{X})\mathrel{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\lnot$}}}d\kappa^{\ast}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}=\left\lparen d\mathcal{X}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\lnot$}}}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}\right\rparen\mathbin{\circ}\pi_{0}+\vertlift(\mathcal{X})\mathrel{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\lnot$}}}d\kappa^{\ast}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}

\displaystyle=\left\lparen d\mathcal{X}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\lnot$}}}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}\right\rparen\mathbin{\circ}\pi_{0}+\left\lparen\mathcal{X}\mathrel{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\lnot$}}}d\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}\right\rparen\mathbin{\circ}\pi_{0}=\left\lparen\mathop{\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{X}}}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}\right\rparen\mathbin{\circ}\pi_{0}.

Thus, the statement follows;
this can locally be reused for the case of a 1
-
density,
cf. the proof of
[
Nic96
, Proposition 3.4.3]
.
Combining the results for gradient and divergence
we get the equation for the Laplace-Beltrami operator.

Finally, let
\mathcal{Y}\in\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\textstyle\Gamma^{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}{\scriptstyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma^{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}};\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\right\rparen}
.
Recall that
the Sasakian volume measure is realised by a volume form
\mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{s}}}}

which could be written as wedge product of a vertical and horizontal volume form:

\mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{s}}}}=\mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{h}}}}
.
Then, the divergence wrt. Sasaki metric is characterised by

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{Y}}}\mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{s}}}}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{Y}}}\left\lparen\mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{h}}}}\right\rparen=\mathop{\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{Y}}}\mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{h}}}}+\mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\wedge\mathop{\mathscr{L}_{\mathcal{Y}}}\mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{h}}}}

(2.2)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} =\mathop{\mathscr{L}_{\mathord{\mathrm{vpr}}(\mathcal{Y})}}\mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{h}}}}+\mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\wedge\mathop{\mathscr{L}_{\mathord{\mathrm{hpr}}(\mathcal{Y})}}\mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{h}}}}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle=\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{div}_{\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}(\mathord{\mathrm{vpr}}(\mathcal{Y}))+\mathord{\mathrm{div}_{\mathord{\mathrm{h}}}}(\mathord{\mathrm{hpr}}(\mathcal{Y}))\right\rparen\mathbin{\cdot}\mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\wedge\mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{h}}}}=\mathord{\mathrm{div}_{\mathord{\mathrm{s}}}}(\mathcal{Y})\mathbin{\cdot}\mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{s}}}},

where we used the local coordinate description of Lie derivatives in line (
2.2
).
∎

Remark 2.32

(Sasaki gradient of loc-density)
.

Basically, the Sasakian gradient of
a tangent bundle weighting
\rho=\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}\rho_{F}

decomposes as

\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{s}}}}\rho=\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}^{\mathrm{v}}\mathbin{\cdot}\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\rho_{F}\oplus\rho_{F}\mathbin{\cdot}\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{h}}}}\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}^{\mathrm{v}}

reminiscent of the Leibniz rule.
Here, we think of
\rho_{F}
as an independent copy defined on the pointwise tangent spaces.
Actually, the symbol

\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\rho_{F}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathord{\mathrm{vpr}}(\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{s}}}}\rho)

is an intuitive short hand for the vertical component of the local vector field

\varphi_{2}^{-1}(\mathord{\mathrm{Id}},\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathrm{euc}}}\rho_{F}\mathbin{\circ}\proj_{2}\mathbin{\circ}\varphi_{1})
,
where the diffeomorphisms
\varphi_{1}
,
\varphi_{2}
render the diagram
in
Figure 2
commutative.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}^{-1}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}^{-1}\left\lparen U\times\mathbb{R}^{\text{n}}\right\rparen}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}^{-1}(U)\times\mathbb{R}^{\text{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}^{-1}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\times\mathbb{R}^{\text{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{0},\mathord{\mathrm{Id}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\proj_{1}}

Figure 2.
Local trivialisation reflected in the double tangent bundle

∎

We define the basic concept of semisprays
which arises in the abstract study of second order ordinary differential equations –
particularly, of Newton’s (second) law on manifolds, see
[
Gli97
, Section 4]
–,
and also encodes geometric extra structure in several interesting ways.
As a consequence of the discussion,
the direct sum
\mathcal{V}\oplus\mathcal{H}
of canonical vector field and semispray
\mathcal{H}
is understood as the diagonal mapping

\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\to\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}},\ v\longmapsto\vertlift(v)\oplus\horlift(v).

Definition 2.33

(Semispray)
.

A section
\mathcal{H}\in\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\textstyle\Gamma^{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}{\scriptstyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma^{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}};\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\right\rparen}

is a
semispray
if it satisfies

\mathord{\left\langle\mathcal{H}\mathbin{,}d\pi_{0}\right\rangle}=\mathord{\mathrm{Id}}_{\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}

or equivalently if
any integral curve
s\colon\,\mathord{\mathbb{I}}\to\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}

takes the form
s=(\pi_{0}\mathbin{\circ}s)^{\prime}
.
A curve
c\colon\,\mathord{\mathbb{I}}\to\mathord{\mathbb{M}}
is called
geodesic of the semispray
\mathcal{H}

if
there is an integral curve
s\colon\,\mathord{\mathbb{I}}\to\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}

such that
c=\pi_{0}\mathbin{\circ}s
.
Equivalently,
c
is geodesic if
\mathcal{H}\mathbin{\circ}c^{\prime}=c^{\prime\prime}
.
For a local coordinate form of semisprays
see the
Appendix A
.
∎

Example 2.34

(Semispray associated to an Ehresmann connection)
.

Consider an Ehresmann connection

\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}=\mathord{\mathrm{V}\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}\oplus\mathord{\mathrm{H}\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}
.
Then, given
v\in\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}
there is a unique horizontal vector
a
–
namely the horizontal lift of
v
–
such that
v=\mathord{\left\langle a\mathbin{,}d\pi_{0}\right\rangle}
.
Furthermore,
a
depends on
v
smoothly.
Hence, there is
\mathcal{H}\in\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\textstyle\Gamma^{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}{\scriptstyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma^{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}};\mathord{\mathrm{H}\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}\right\rparen}

such that
\mathord{\mathrm{Id}}_{\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}=\mathord{\left\langle\mathcal{H}\mathbin{,}d\pi_{0}\right\rangle}
.
Then,
\mathcal{H}
is a semispray
and we say that it is the
semispray associated to the Ehresmann connection
.
In particular, there is the semispray
\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}

associated to the Riemann metric
\mathord{\mathrm{m}}
via the corresponding Ehresmann connection.
We call it
Riemannian semispray
.

Another common name is
geodesic spray
,
since it can be constructed just in terms of geodesics corresponding to the Riemannian metric,
see
[
Car92
, Lemma III.2.3]
.
So, the geodesics of this semispray are just classical geodesics.
In fact, this semispray satisfies a homogeneity condition making it a (full) spray
whence the name –
we will use the spray structure a few times indeed.
∎

Remark 2.35
.

In a nutshell, choosing an Ehresmann connection adds the same geometric information
as choosing a covariant derivative or a semispray.
See
[
Buc12
]
and various references therein.
We think that this observation might be a good starting point for
generalisations even to rough geometries,
as it translates well to Lagrange and Finsler spaces.
∎

Example 2.36

(Semisprays induced by Lagrangians)
.

Let the Lagrangian
\mathsf{L}=\frac{1}{2}\,\lvert\cdot\rvert_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}^{2}
.
Then, the Lagrangian vector field
\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{L}}

is a semispray
5
5

5

In fact, it is the geodesic spray again.
,
see
[
MR99
, Section 7.5]
.
As outlined in
[
Lan95
, Section VII.6]

this relation can be translated to general Riemannian manifolds modelled on some Hilbert space.
Note that existence of such a vector field is due to
the more general result
[
Lan95
, Proposition VII.5.9]
:
Let
\mathsf{Q}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}
,
then the cotangent space of
\mathsf{Q}
naturally
6
6

6

Up to sign conventions:
Let
\Theta
denote the canonical 1
-
form,
then we define the 2
-
form
\Omega
by
\Omega=-d\Theta
.

yields the structure of a symplectic manifold

(\mathord{\mathrm{T}^{\ast}\mathsf{Q}},\Omega)
.
Furthermore, we make
\mathsf{Q}
into a Riemannian manifold
endowing it with a ‘natural metric’ in the sense of Gudmundsson-Kappos.
This specifies the musical isomorphisms, in particular
\sharp
.
Thus, there is a unique section

\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{L}}\in\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\textstyle\Gamma^{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}{\scriptstyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma^{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathsf{Q}}\right\rparen}
such that

\left\lparen\Omega^{\sharp}\mathbin{\circ}\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{L}}\right\rparen_{w}(a)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\Omega_{w}^{\sharp}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{L}}(w),a)=d_{w}\mathsf{L}(a)\qquad\text{for all }w\in\mathsf{Q},a\in\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{w}}{\textstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle w$}}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{w}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle w$}}}\mathsf{Q}},

where
\Omega^{\sharp}
denotes the pullback of
\Omega
wrt.
\sharp
.
With that, we can say that

\mathcal{H}_{\mathsf{L}}=(d\,\mathsf{L})^{\sharp}=\mathord{\nabla}\mathsf{L}
,
where the gradient is taken wrt. the ‘natural’ metric chosen before.
See
[
Buc06
, Section 3]

for another short discussion of semisprays on general Lagrange spaces.
∎

Remark 2.37

(Revisiting
Example
2.34
)
.

We shall characterise the Riemannian semispray using
[
Lan95
, Proposition VII.5.9]

similar to the previous example.
Again, let
\mathsf{Q}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}

and the symplectic manifold
(\mathord{\mathrm{T}^{\ast}\mathsf{Q}},\Omega)
.
Let
\mathord{\mathrm{h}}=\pi_{0}^{\ast}\mathord{\mathrm{m}}
be the horizontal metric,
which is the simplest ‘natural’ metric,
and denote the pullback of the canonical 2
-
form
\Omega
wrt. this metric by
\Omega^{\mathrm{h}}
.
Locally we can think of

a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{w}^{\ast}}{\textstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle w$}}^{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\ast$}}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{w}^{\ast}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle w$}}^{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\ast$}}}\mathsf{Q}}
,
w\in\mathsf{Q}
,
as tuples
a^{\prime}=(u_{1},u_{2}^{\prime})
and
b^{\prime}=(v_{1},v_{2}^{\prime})

with
u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2}\in\mathsf{Q}

and

u_{2}^{\prime}=\mathord{\mathrm{m}_{x}\left\lparen\cdot\mathbin{,}u_{2}\right\rparen},v_{2}^{\prime}=\mathord{\mathrm{m}_{x}\left\lparen\cdot\mathbin{,}v_{2}\right\rparen}\in\mathsf{Q}^{\ast}
,
where
x\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\pi_{0}(w)
.
Then, the form
\Omega^{\mathrm{h}}
reads as

\displaystyle\Omega^{\mathrm{h}}_{w}((u_{1},u_{2}),(v_{1},v_{2}))

\displaystyle=\mathord{\mathrm{m}_{x}\left\lparen u_{1}\mathbin{,}v_{2}\right\rparen}-\mathord{\mathrm{m}_{x}\left\lparen v_{1}\mathbin{,}u_{2}\right\rparen}

\displaystyle=\mathord{\left\langle u_{1}\mathbin{,}v_{2}^{\prime}\right\rangle}-\mathord{\left\langle v_{1}\mathbin{,}u_{2}^{\prime}\right\rangle}=\Omega_{w}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime}).

There is a 1
-
form
\omega

which reads in such local regimes as

\omega_{w}(b)=\omega_{w}(v_{1},v_{2})=\mathord{\mathrm{m}_{\pi_{0}(w)}\left\lparen w\mathbin{,}v_{1}-v_{2}\right\rparen}
.
The unique section
\mathcal{H}\in\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\textstyle\Gamma^{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}{\scriptstyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma^{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathsf{Q}}\right\rparen}

such that
\Omega^{\mathrm{h}}\mathbin{\circ}\mathcal{H}=\omega

is the Riemannian semispray.
∎

Example 2.38

(Euclidean case)
.

The Riemannian semispray
\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}

acts on vertically lifted functions
f=f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}
with
f_{0}\in\mathord{C^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\right\rparen}
as

\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}f=\mathord{\mathrm{h}\left\lparen\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}\mathbin{,}\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{h}}}}f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}\right\rparen}=\mathord{\mathrm{m}_{\pi_{0}}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{Id}}_{\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}\mathbin{,}\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}f_{0}\mathbin{\circ}\pi_{0}\right\rparen}.

In case of
\mathord{\mathbb{M}}=\mathbb{R}^{\text{n}}
with standard Riemannian metric
this action is written as

\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{euc}}f(x,v)=\left\lparen v\mathbin{,}\nabla_{x}f_{0}(x)\right\rparen_{\mathrm{euc}}

for smooth functions

f\colon\,\mathbb{R}^{\text{n}}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{\text{n}}_{v}\to\mathbb{R},\,(x,v)\mapsto f_{0}(x)

and
x,v\in\mathbb{R}^{\text{n}}
.

By analogy, the canonical vector field
\mathcal{V}
acts on
horizontally lifted functions
f=f_{0}^{\mathrm{h}}
with
f_{0}\in\mathord{C^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\right\rparen}
as

\mathcal{V}f=\mathord{\mathrm{v}\left\lparen\mathcal{V}\mathbin{,}\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}f_{0}^{\mathrm{h}}\right\rparen}=\mathord{\mathrm{m}_{\pi_{0}}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{Id}}_{\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}\mathbin{,}\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}f_{0}\mathbin{\circ}\pi_{0}\right\rparen}.

In the Euclidean case,
this action is written as

\mathcal{V}f(x,v)=\left\lparen v\mathbin{,}\nabla_{v}f_{0}(v)\right\rparen_{\mathrm{euc}}

for smooth functions

f\colon\,\mathbb{R}^{\text{n}}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{\text{n}}_{v}\to\mathbb{R},\,(x,v)\mapsto f_{0}(v)

and
x,v\in\mathbb{R}^{\text{n}}
.
∎

The following theorem is well-known,
but comes in a few quite different formulations e. g.:
The geodesic flow preserves the volume of
\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}
.
We just stick to the formulation below,
since it tells us that
the Riemannian semispray is an antisymmetric operator
wrt.
\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}};\mathord{\mathrm{s}}\right\rparen}
-
scalar product.

Theorem 2.39

(Liouville’s Theorem)
.

The semispray
\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}
is solenoidal wrt. Sasaki metric
\mathord{\mathrm{s}}
.

Proof.

A proof could be done via
explicitly calculating the Sasakian volume form in normal coordinates.
This approach is taken from the neat book by M. do Carmo,
cf.
[
Car92
, Exercise 3.14]
.
∎

The final lemma of this section
characterises the test functions on the tangent space
exploiting again the ‘almost product’ structure due to local trivialisation.
This result will be very important arguing
for a reasonable set of test functions being a core of the (Langevin/fibre lay-down) generator.

Lemma 2.40

(Tensor product of lifted test functions)
.

Define the tensor product space of pulled back test functions

\displaystyle D_{0}

\displaystyle{}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\pi_{0}^{\ast}\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\right\rparen}\mathbin{\otimes}\kappa^{\ast}\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\right\rparen}

\displaystyle=\linhull\left\{\pi_{0}^{\ast}f_{0}\mathbin{\otimes}\kappa^{\ast}g_{0}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\pi_{0}^{\ast}f_{0}\mathbin{\cdot}\kappa^{\ast}g_{0}\ \middle|\ f_{0},g_{0}\in\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\right\rparen}\right\}

\displaystyle{}=\linhull\left\{f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}\mathbin{\otimes}g_{0}^{\mathrm{h}}\ \middle|\ f_{0},g_{0}\in\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\right\rparen}\right\}.

Then,
D_{0}
is dense
7
7

7

Dense wrt. the usual locally convex topology induced by appropriate seminorms,
which implies uniform convergence of all derivatives on compacts;
for the well-known Euclidean case see
[
Hor66
, Example 2.4.10]

and for the generalised geometric case see
[
GKOS01
, Section 3.1.3]
.
In common notation for test function spaces with this topology,
the result reads as

\pi_{0}^{\ast}\mathcal{D}(\mathord{\mathbb{M}})\mathbin{\otimes}\kappa^{\ast}\mathcal{D}(\mathord{\mathbb{M}})

is dense in

\mathcal{D}(\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}})
.
By the way,
the authors of
[
GKOS01
]
not only discuss generalised distribution spaces rigorously,
they shed some light on integration of 1
-
densities from a very analytic perspective.

in
\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\right\rparen}
.

Proof.

Consider a local trivialisation in
U\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=U_{x}\cap W

for a chart domain
W\subseteq\mathord{\mathbb{M}}
at
x\in\mathord{\mathbb{M}}

with
V\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\pi_{0}^{-1}(U)
and the diffeomorphism
\varphi

rendering the diagram in
Figure 1
commutative.
Then, we immediately conclude the relations

\pi_{0}^{\ast}\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen U\right\rparen}=\varphi^{\ast}\left\lparen\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen U\right\rparen}\mathbin{\otimes}\{1\}\right\rparen\qquad\text{and}\qquad\kappa^{\ast}\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen U\right\rparen}=\varphi^{\ast}\left\lparen\{1\}\mathbin{\otimes}\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathbb{R}^{\text{n}}\right\rparen}\right\rparen.

Thus, by
[
Hor66
, Proposition 4.8.1]
the tensor product

\pi_{0}^{\ast}\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen U\right\rparen}\mathbin{\otimes}\kappa^{\ast}\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen U\right\rparen}

is dense in
\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen V\right\rparen}=\varphi^{\ast}\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen U\times\mathbb{R}^{\text{n}}\right\rparen}

and the proof is finished via a partition of unity argument.
∎

3.
Hypocoercivity for geometric Langevin dynamics

In this section we apply the abstract Hilbert space hypocoercivity method
to the Langevin equation with some Riemannian manifold as position space,
see
Theorem
3.2
below.
That is the direct generalisation of the situation of
[
GS16
]
.
The techniques of proving we learned from
[
GS14
]
.
Our interest arose from industrial fibre lay-down applications
and qualitative analysis of the nonwoven.
In this context, the position manifold
\mathord{\mathbb{M}}
could e. g. reflect a sagging conveyor belt
or a belt moving over a cylindrical roller.
As we mentioned in the introduction,
Langevin dynamics have wide ranging applications
and our approach offers the freedom to include any ‘smooth’ side condition on the position variable.

Consider the following Stratonovich SDE in
\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}
:

(3.1)

\mathrm{d}\eta=\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}\ \mathrm{d}t+\vertlift_{\eta}(-\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}\Psi)\ \mathrm{d}t+\sigma\mathbin{\cdot}\vertlift_{\eta}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{\text{n}}\frac{\partial}{\partial{x_{\eta}^{j}}}\right)\mathbin{\circ}\mathrm{d}W_{t}-\alpha\mathbin{\cdot}\mathcal{V}\ \mathrm{d}t,

where
\eta\colon\,\mathord{\mathbb{I}}\to\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}
is a curve with time interval
\mathord{\mathbb{I}}
and
\left\lparen x_{\eta}^{1},x_{\eta}^{2},\ldots,x_{\eta}^{\text{n}}\right\rparen
is a chart at
\pi_{0}(\eta)

providing normal coordinates.
Recall that
\mathcal{V}
denotes the canonical vector field.
Bellow we specify certain assumptions on the potential
\Psi\colon\,\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathbb{R}
.
The nonnegative model parameters
\alpha
and
\sigma
are related by

\sigma=\sqrt{\nicefrac{{2\alpha}}{{\beta}}}
,
where
\beta
is a nonnegative rescaling of the potential as
\Phi=\beta\Psi
.
Recall that
the horizontal motion in
Equation (
3.1
)
, i. e.
\mathord{\mathrm{hpr}}(\mathrm{d}\eta)=\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}\ \mathrm{d}t
,
reflects the natural requirement of

(\pi_{0}\mathbin{\circ}\eta)^{\prime}=\eta

which is the form that any integral curve of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}
attains by definition.
We call
Equation (
3.1
)

the
Langevin equation on
\mathord{\mathbb{M}}

or just
geometric Langevin equation
.

Using
[
WI81
, Theorem V.1.2]

the Kolmogorov generator
L
is given as

(3.2)

L=\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}-\frac{1}{\beta}\,\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\Phi^{\mathrm{h}}+\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\,\mathop{\Delta_{\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}-\alpha\mathcal{V}.

This operator is defined for all smooth functions on
\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}
,
but we consider the domain
D\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\right\rparen}
of smooth test functions
with compact support.
Note that
(L,D)
is densely defined
by
Remark
2.9
.
We usually call
L
as in
Equation (
3.2
)

the
Langevin generator
.
Compare this generator to
[
GS16
, Equation (1.2)]

taking
Example
2.38
into account.
As we will see in
Lemma
3.4
,
the Langevin generator basically decomposes into two components:
the vertical diffusion
and the (not entirely horizontal) component liaising the appropriate notion of second order differential equations.
Compare this decomposition to so-called hypoelliptic Laplacians;
via this concept J.-M. Bismut links Brownian motion on manifolds and geodesic flow
in order to find a Langevin process in
[
Bis15
]
.

Before we start checking the data and hypocoercivity conditions,
we shall fix conditions on the potential in the geometric Langevin equation,
and thus on the base weight

\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}=\exp(-\Phi)=\exp(-\beta\Psi)
.

Condition 3.1

(Potential conditions
3.1
)
.

(P1)

General regularity and boundedness:
Let
\Phi=\beta\Psi
a loc
-
Lipschitzian potential
which is bounded from below
and such that

\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}}}=\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\,\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}=\exp(-\Phi)\,\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}

is a probability measure on
(\mathord{\mathbb{M}},\mathop{\mathfrak{B}(\mathord{\mathbb{M}})})
.

(P2)

Poincaré inequality:
The weighted Riemannian measure
\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}}}

satisfies the Poincaré inequality

(3.3)

\lVert\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}f_{0}\rVert_{\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}};\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}\right\rparen}}^{2}\geq\Lambda\lVert f_{0}-\left\lparen f_{0}\mathbin{,}1\right\rparen_{\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}};\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}\right\rparen}}\rVert_{\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}};\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}\right\rparen}}^{2}

for all
f_{0}\in\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\right\rparen}

and some
\Lambda\in(0,\infty)
.

(P3)

Hessian dominated by gradient:
Assume
\Phi\in\mathord{C^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\right\rparen}
.
There is a constant
c\in(0,\infty)
such that

\lvert\operatorname{Hess}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}(\Phi)(x)\rvert\leq c\,(1+\lvert\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}\Phi(x)\rvert_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}})\qquad\text{holds for all }x\in\mathord{\mathbb{M}}.

Here, ‘
\operatorname{Hess}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}(\cdot)
’
denotes the Hessian wrt. the given Riemannian metric
and the norm ‘
\lvert\operatorname{Hess}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}(\cdot)\rvert
’
of the Hessian is the Frobenius tensor norm induced by the Riemannian metric.

∎

As explained in
[
GS14
, Remark 3.16]

the condition
(P3)
as above can be weakened.
Clearly, the
Poincaré inequality (
3.3
)

is the most restrictive condition wrt. the geometry of the weighted position manifold
(\mathord{\mathbb{M}},\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}})
.
It’s satisfied for compact manifolds, see
[
Heb99
, Theorems 2.10, 2.11]
.
For the noncompact case we refer to
[
Heb99
, Lemma 3.1]
,
where the necessary geometric assumption is that
the Ricci curvature is bounded from below by some multiple of
\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}
.
Furthermore, we point out that
in
Lemma
3.4

the nasty assumption of a weakly harmonic potential appears.
In view of Weyl’s theorem it would be quite a restrictive assumption.
That’s why
in
Proposition
3.7

we remove this assumption
for the general setting of this section.
Note that by
Lemma
B.1

the Riemannian manifold
(\mathord{\mathbb{M}},\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}})
weighted by a potential as above
is complete again.

We formulate the main theorem of this section:

Theorem 3.2

(Hypocoercivity of the geometric Langevin dynamic)
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in(0,\infty)

and
(\mathord{\mathbb{M}},\mathord{\mathrm{m}})
be a Riemannian manifold satisfying
2.1
.
We assume that the potential
\Phi\colon\,\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathbb{R}

fulfils the conditions
3.1
above.
Denote by
\nu
the zero-mean Gaussian measure with covariance matrix
\beta^{-1}\mathord{\mathrm{Id}}

and define

\mu\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}}}\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}\nu
.

Then, the Langevin operator

\left\lparen L,\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\right\rparen}\right\rparen=\left\lparen\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\,\mathop{\Delta_{\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}-\alpha\mathcal{V}+\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}-\frac{1}{\beta}\,\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\Phi^{\mathrm{h}},\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\right\rparen}\right\rparen

is closable in
H\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}};\mu\right\rparen}
.
Moreover, its closure
\mathord{\left\lparen L,\mathord{D\left\lparen L\right\rparen}\right\rparen}
generates a strongly continuous contraction semigroup
(T_{t})_{t\in[0,\infty)}
.
Finally, there are constants
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\in(0,\infty)

computable in terms of
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\Lambda
and
c

such that for all
g\in H
and times
t\in[0,\infty)
holds

\lVert T_{t}g-\left\lparen g\mathbin{,}1\right\rparen_{H}\rVert_{H}\leq\kappa_{1}\mathord{\mathrm{e}}^{-\kappa_{2}t}\lVert g-\left\lparen g\mathbin{,}1\right\rparen_{H}\rVert_{H}.

Clearly, we are going to prove it by applying the
2.2
.

3.1.
Data conditions

Definition 3.3

(model Hilbert space
(D1)
)
.

Consider the probability space

(E,\mathfrak{E},\mu)=\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}},\mathop{\mathfrak{B}(\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}})},\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}}}\right\rparen,

where
\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}}}=\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}}}\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}\nu

is the weighted Sasaki volume measure with

\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}
is weighted by

\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\exp(-\Phi)=\exp(-\beta\Psi)
with
\beta\in(0,\infty)
such that

\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}}}
is a probability measure on
(\mathord{\mathbb{M}},\mathop{\mathfrak{B}(\mathord{\mathbb{M}})})
,
and
\nu=\mathord{\mathrm{N}\left\lparen 0;\beta^{-1}\mathord{\mathrm{Id}}_{\text{n}}\right\rparen}

is the zero-mean normal distribution on the fibre
F=\mathbb{R}^{\text{n}}

with covariance matrix
\beta^{-1}\mathord{\mathrm{Id}}_{\text{n}}
.
In other words,

\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}}}
has the loc
-
density

\exp(-\Phi)\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}\prod_{j=1}^{\text{n}}\,\varphi_{0,\beta^{-1}}\mathbin{\circ}\proj_{j}
,
where
\varphi_{0,\beta^{-1}}
denotes the density of a one-dimensional normal distribution with variance
\beta^{-1}
.
The model Hilbert space is

H\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen E;\mu\right\rparen}=\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}};\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}\right\rparen}
,
cf.
Notation
2.16
.
∎

Lemma 3.4

(SAD-decomposition
(D3)
,
(D4)
,
(D6)
)
.

Let condition
(P1)
hold and let
\Phi
be weakly harmonic.
Consider the SAD-decomposition
L=S-A
on
D
with

\displaystyle Sf

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\,\mathop{\Delta_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}}}f=\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\,\mathop{\Delta_{\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}f-\alpha\mathcal{V}f

\displaystyle\quad\text{and}\quad Af

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}}f\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=-\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}f+\frac{1}{\beta}\,\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\Phi^{\mathrm{h}}\right\rparen(f)

for all
f\in D
.
Then, the following assertions hold:

(i)

(S,D)
is symmetric and negative semidefinite.

(ii)

(A,D)
is antisymmetric.

(iii)

For all
f\in D
we have that

Lf\in\mathord{L^{1}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}};\mu\right\rparen}
and

\int_{\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}Lf\ \mathrm{d}\mu=0
.

Proof.

(i)

Combining the form of
\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{s}}}}\rho

from
Remark
2.32

with the result on the weighted Laplace-Beltrami,
see
Lemma
2.18
,
we know that
we have to look at
\frac{1}{\rho_{F}}\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\rho_{F}
.
Condensing notation a bit
we calculate that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\rho_{F}}\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\rho_{F}(f)

\displaystyle=\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathrm{euc}}}\left\lparen-\frac{\beta}{2}\,\lvert\mathord{\mathrm{Id}}_{F}\rvert_{\mathrm{euc}}^{2}\right\rparen(f)=-\beta\mathord{\left\langle\mathord{\mathrm{Id}}_{\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}\mathbin{,}df_{0}\right\rangle}=-\beta\mathbin{\cdot}\mathcal{V}f

holds for all
f=f_{0}^{\mathrm{h}}\in\kappa^{\ast}\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\right\rparen}
.
Hence, it follows

\left\lparen Sf\mathbin{,}g\right\rparen_{H}=-\int_{\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}\mathord{\mathrm{v}\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}f\mathbin{,}\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}g\right\rparen}\ \mathrm{d}\mu\qquad\text{for all }f,g\in D_{0},

and therefore
(S,D_{0})
is symmetric and nonpositive definite.
Since
S
is well-defined on
D
and
D_{0}
is dense in
D
wrt. graph norm,
part
(i)
follows directly.

(ii)

By
2.39

and similar reasoning as in
Remark
2.17

we know the adjoint of the Riemannian semispray wrt.
\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}};\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}\right\rparen}
-
scalar product:

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}^{\ast}}{\textstyle\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}^{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\ast$}}}{\scriptstyle\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}^{\ast}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}^{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\ast$}}}

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}-0-\frac{1}{\rho}\,\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}\rho=-\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}+\frac{1}{\rho_{F}}\,\beta\rho_{F}\mathbin{\cdot}\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}\Psi^{\mathrm{v}}

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}+\beta\mathbin{\cdot}\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}\Psi^{\mathrm{v}}.

Furthermore, we compute the adjoint wrt.
\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}};\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}\right\rparen}
-
scalar product
of
\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\Psi^{\mathrm{h}}=\frac{1}{\beta}\,\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\Phi^{\mathrm{h}}

using that
\Psi
is weakly harmonic
and adapting
Remark
2.32
accordingly:

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\displaystyle\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\Psi^{\mathrm{h}}\right\rparen^{\ast}}{\textstyle\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\Psi^{\mathrm{h}}\right\rparen^{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\ast$}}}{\scriptstyle\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\Psi^{\mathrm{h}}\right\rparen^{\ast}}{\scriptscriptstyle\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\Psi^{\mathrm{h}}\right\rparen^{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\ast$}}}

\displaystyle=-\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\Psi^{\mathrm{h}}-\underbrace{\mathord{\mathrm{div}_{\mathord{\mathrm{s}}}}\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\Psi^{\mathrm{h}}\right\rparen}_{=0}-\frac{1}{\rho}\,\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\Psi^{\mathrm{h}}\right\rparen(\rho)

\displaystyle=-\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\Psi^{\mathrm{h}}-\frac{1}{\rho}\,\rho_{\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}^{\mathrm{v}}\mathbin{\cdot}\underbrace{\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\Psi^{\mathrm{h}}\right\rparen(\rho_{F})}_{=\,\mathord{\mathrm{v}\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\Psi^{\mathrm{h}}\mathbin{,}\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\rho_{F}\right\rparen}}

\displaystyle=-\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\Psi^{\mathrm{h}}-\underbrace{\mathord{\mathrm{v}\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\Psi^{\mathrm{h}}\mathbin{,}-\beta\mathcal{V}\right\rparen}}_{=\,\mathord{\mathrm{h}\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{h}}}}\Psi^{\mathrm{v}}\mathbin{,}-\beta\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}\right\rparen}}=-\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\Psi^{\mathrm{h}}+\beta\mathbin{\cdot}\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}\Psi^{\mathrm{v}}.

Hence,
(A,D)
is antisymmetric.
Since
(A,D)
is densely defined on
H
,
it is closable.

(iii)

The integrability statement is clear:
Let
h\in D
arbitrary,
then we conclude that

\int_{\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}Sh\,\mathrm{d}\mu=0

by part
(i)

and

\int_{\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}Ah\,\mathrm{d}\mu=0

by part
(ii)
.
We finish the proof pointing out
that
(L,D)
is densely defined on
H
and dissipative,
thus it is closable.

∎

Notation 3.5
.

Due
Lemma
3.4

(S,D)
,
(A,D)
and
(L,D)
are closable.
The closures we denote by
\mathord{\left\lparen S,\mathord{D\left\lparen S\right\rparen}\right\rparen}
,
\mathord{\left\lparen A,\mathord{D\left\lparen A\right\rparen}\right\rparen}
and
\mathord{\left\lparen L,\mathord{D\left\lparen L\right\rparen}\right\rparen}
respectively.
∎

From the easiest example of Euclidean space
we learn that one actually doesn’t need a weakly harmonic potential.
To see this, we make use of Poisson manifolds.

Example 3.6
.

Let
\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\mathbb{R}_{x}^{\text{n}}
.
Then, the inverse
\begin{pmatrix}0&-\mathord{\mathrm{Id}_{\text{n}}}\\
\mathord{\mathrm{Id}_{\text{n}}}&0\end{pmatrix}

of ‘the’ symplectic matrix yields an almost complex structure
\mathbb{J}
on

\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathbb{R}_{x}^{\text{n}}}\simeq\mathbb{R}_{x}^{\text{n}}\times\mathbb{R}_{v}^{\text{n}}
.
It’s the same as the one constructed
in
[
Dom62
, Paragraph 5]
or later in
[
TO62
]
,
by our convention of listing the vertical component first and the horizontal one second.
Moreover, it is
compatible
with the Euclidean metric
and the canonical symplectic form
\Omega
on

\mathord{\mathrm{T}^{\ast}\mathbb{R}_{x}^{\text{n}}}\simeq\mathbb{R}_{x}^{\text{n}}\times\left\lparen\mathbb{R}^{\text{n}}\right\rparen^{\ast}

in the sense that

\left\lparen v\mathbin{,}w\right\rparen_{\mathrm{euc}}=\Omega(v,\mathbb{J}w)\quad\text{and}\quad\Omega(v,w)=\left\lparen\mathbb{J}v\mathbin{,}w\right\rparen_{\mathrm{euc}}\qquad\text{for all }v,w\in\mathbb{R}^{2\text{n}},

cf.
[
MR99
, Exercise 2.2.1]
.
The symplectic form gives rise to a Poisson bracket
\mathord{\left\{\cdot\mathbin{,}\cdot\right\}}

such that the correpsonding Poisson tensor reads as

\mathord{\left\{f\mathbin{,}g\right\}}(x,v)=-\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathrm{euc}}}f(x,v)\mathbin{,}\mathbb{J}\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathrm{euc}}}g(x,v)\right\rparen_{\mathrm{euc}}=\Omega_{(x,v)}(df,dg)

for all
f,g\in\mathord{C^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathbb{R}^{2\text{n}}\right\rparen}
and
(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}_{x}^{\text{n}}\times\mathbb{R}_{v}^{\text{n}}
.

In this terminology, the proof of
[
GS16
, Lemma 3.4 part ii)]
relies on
linking the operator
(A,D)
to the antisymmetric bilinear form
(\mathcal{A},D)

of integrating minus the Poisson bracket wrt.
\mu
:

\mathcal{A}(f,g)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2\text{n}}}-\mathord{\left\{f\mathbin{,}g\right\}}\ \mathrm{d}\mu

for all
f,g\in D=\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathbb{R}^{2\text{n}}\right\rparen}
.
Via integration by parts one can show that

\left\lparen Af\mathbin{,}g\right\rparen_{\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mu\right\rparen}}=\mathcal{A}(f,g)
holds for all
f,g\in\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathbb{R}^{2\text{n}}\right\rparen}
.
This can be done
without the assumption
of a weakly harmonic potential,
since the vector field action can be represented in terms of the Hamiltonian vector fields.
Indeed:
Denote by
\mathsf{H}_{f}
the
Hamiltonian vector field
of
f\in\mathord{C^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathbb{R}^{2\text{n}}\right\rparen}
, i. e. it fulfils
\mathsf{H}_{f}(g)=\mathord{\left\{f\mathbin{,}g\right\}}

for all
g\in\mathord{C^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathbb{R}^{2\text{n}}\right\rparen}
.
We get the explicit formula

-\mathsf{H}_{f}=\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0muv}}f\mathbin{,}\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mux}}\right\rparen_{\mathrm{euc}}-\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mux}}f\mathbin{,}\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0muv}}\right\rparen_{\mathrm{euc}}

and conclude that
\mathsf{H}_{f}
is solenoidal by Schwarz’s theorem.
Hence, the following equality is true:

\mathcal{A}(f,g)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2\text{n}}}\mathsf{H}_{f}(\rho)\mathbin{\cdot}g\ \mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda}=\left\lparen Af\mathbin{,}g\right\rparen_{\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mu\right\rparen}}\qquad\text{for all }f,g\in D,

where
\rho\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\frac{d\mu}{d\mathord{\lambda}}
is the (loc-)density of
\mu
.
∎

Proposition 3.7

(SAD-decomposition (2nd version))
.

The assertions of
Lemma
3.4

are true without the assumption of
\Psi
being weakly harmonic.

Proof.

We enhance the proof of
Lemma
3.4
part
(ii)

via the technique discussed in the previous example
which works due to the particular choices of
the Gaussian fibre measure and the Sasaki metric wrt. the most natural Ehresmann connection on
\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}
.

Let’s abbreviate by

\rho\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\frac{d\mu}{d\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{s}}}}}=\frac{d\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}}}}{d\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{s}}}}}

the (loc-)density of
\mu
.
Denote by
\mathbb{J}
minus the almost complex structure on
\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}
constructed in
[
Dom62
]
,
and let
\Omega
be the canonical symplectic form on
\mathord{\mathrm{T}^{\ast}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}
.
By construction, the Sasaki metric,
\mathbb{J}
and
\Omega
are compatible,
cf.
[
MR99
, page 341]
.
Hence, they define the same Poisson bracket
\mathord{\left\{\cdot\mathbin{,}\cdot\right\}}
on
\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}
via the assignments

\Omega_{v}(df,dg)=\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}\mathord{\left\{f\mathbin{,}g\right\}}(v)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=-\mathord{\mathrm{s}_{v}\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{s}}}}f(v)\mathbin{,}\mathbb{J}\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{s}}}}g(v)\right\rparen}

for all
f,g\in\mathord{C^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\right\rparen}
and
v\in\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}
.
Thus, for any fixed
f\in\mathord{C^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\right\rparen}

there is a unique Hamilton vector field
\mathsf{H}_{f}

by
[
MR99
, Proposition 10.2.1]
.
Let a chart
(v^{j})_{j=1}^{2\text{n}}

that gives normal coordinates and respects the Ehresmann connection
such that
(\partial v^{i})_{i=1}^{\text{n}}
provides local basis for vertical vector fields
and
(\partial v^{k+\text{n}})_{k=1}^{\text{n}}
provides a basis for the horizontal vector fields;
we find that in these coordinates the Hamiltonian vector fields attain the form

-\mathsf{H}_{f}=\sum_{k=1}^{\text{n}}\partial_{v^{k}}f\mathbin{\cdot}\partial_{v^{k+\text{n}}}-\sum_{i=1}^{\text{n}}\partial_{v^{i+\text{n}}}f\mathbin{\cdot}\partial_{v^{i}}.

In any such coordinates we easily compute that

\mathord{\mathrm{div}_{\mathord{\mathrm{s}}}}(\mathsf{H}_{f})=0
using Schwarz’s Theorem,
in other words all
\mathsf{H}_{f}
are solenoidal.

Define the antisymmetric bilinear form
(\mathcal{A},D)
by

\mathcal{A}(f,g)\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\int_{\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}-\mathord{\left\{f\mathbin{,}g\right\}}\ \mathrm{d}\mu

for all
f,g\in D=\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\right\rparen}
.
From the Divergence Theorem it follows that

\mathcal{A}(f,g)=\int_{\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}-\mathsf{H}_{f}(g)\ \mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{s}}}}}=\int_{\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}\mathsf{H}_{f}(\rho)\mathbin{\cdot}g\ \mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{s}}}}.

Using our comments on the Sasaki gradient of a loc
-
density,
see
Remark
2.32
,
we can infer that

\mathsf{H}_{f}(\rho)=-\rho\mathbin{\cdot}\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}}f
.
In a nutshell,
we localise in the support of
f

via a partition of unity argument
where the corresponding open cover is formed by
charts
(v^{j})_{j=1}^{2\text{n}}

that are respecting the Ehresmann connection
and also are restricted to domains of local trivialisation;
therein, we can use the local coordinate form of
\mathsf{H}_{f}

and that the loc
-
density
\rho
trivialises to a product of exponential-type densities.
Hence, we gain that

\left\lparen Af\mathbin{,}g\right\rparen_{H}=\mathcal{A}(f,g)
for all
f,g\in D

which finishes the proof.
∎

We are going to construct the projection
P
mentioned in
(D5)
.
For every
f\in H
we call the mapping

\operatorname{E}_{\nu}[f]\colon\ \mathord{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathbb{R},\ x\longmapsto\int_{\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{x}}{\textstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}f\ \mathrm{d}\nu

the
fibrewise average of
f
.
Clearly, the operator
\operatorname{E}_{\nu}
acts trivially on vertically lifted functions,
since they are fibrewise constant:

\operatorname{E}_{\nu}[f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}]=f_{0}

for all
f_{0}\in\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}};\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}\right\rparen}
.
We define
P_{S}f\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\operatorname{E}_{\nu}[f]\mathbin{\circ}\pi_{0}
assigning
to an element of the tangent space the average of
f
over the fibre corresponding to this element.
Thinking in a local trivialisation
the vertical lift of the fibrewise average erases the dependency of
f
on the velocity component,
since
f
can be thought as a bivariate function of position and velocity in this localisation.
The range of
P_{S}
is precisely

P_{S}(H)=\pi_{0}^{\ast}\left\lparen\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}};\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}\right\rparen}\right\rparen
,
the set of vertically lifted
\mathord{L^{2}}
-
functions on the weighted position manifold,
and
P_{S}
is a projection.

Moreover, we have that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}(P_{S}f)^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\mu

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}(\operatorname{E}_{\nu}[f]\mathbin{\circ}\pi_{0})^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}}}\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}\nu=\int_{\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}(\operatorname{E}_{\nu}[f])^{2}\mathbin{\circ}\pi_{0}\ \mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}}}\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}\nu

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}(\operatorname{E}_{\nu}[f])^{2}\mathbin{\circ}\pi_{0}\ \mathrm{d}\pi_{0}^{\ast}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}}}=\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\operatorname{E}_{\nu}[f]^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}}}

for all
f\in H
.
This implies

\lVert P_{S}f\rVert_{H}=\lVert\operatorname{E}_{\nu}[f]\rVert_{\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}};\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}\right\rparen}}\leq\lVert f\rVert_{H}

meaning that
P_{S}
is continuous with norm 1.
Last but not least,
P_{S}
also is an orthogonal projection,
cf.
[
Con90
, Proposition 3.3]
.
Finally, we define
P\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=P_{S}-\left\lparen\cdot\mathbin{,}1\right\rparen_{H}
.

Lemma 3.8

(Properties of projection
P
and semigroup conservativity
(D5)
,
(D7)
)
.

Let condition
(P1)
hold.
Then, we have

P(H)\subseteq\mathord{D\left\lparen S\right\rparen}
,
SP=0
,

P(D)\subseteq\mathord{D\left\lparen A\right\rparen}
and
AP(D)\subseteq\mathord{D\left\lparen A\right\rparen}
.
Furthermore,
1\in\mathord{D\left\lparen L\right\rparen}
and
L1=0
.

Proof.

We follow the lines of the proof of
[
GS16
, Lemma 3.4 (iv)]

for the Euclidean case.

First of all, we start with the statements related to
S
.
Let
\varphi\in\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}};[0,1]\right\rparen}
be a cut-off function
8
8

8

An explicit choice of
\varphi
is given as follows:
Let
h
be a chart at
o
–
wlog. the ball
\mathord{\mathbb{U}(o,2)}
is contained in the chart domain.
Define the auxiliary ‘mountain’ function

m\colon\ \mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},\ t\longmapsto\exp\left\lparen-\frac{1}{t(1-t)}\right\rparen\mathbin{\cdot}\mathord{\mathbbm{1}_{(0,1)}}(t).

We transform the ‘mountain’ to a ‘table mountain’
by the assignment

\tau\colon\ \mathbb{R}^{\text{n}}\to[0,1],\,y\longmapsto\frac{\int_{0}^{2-\lVert y\rVert_{2}}m(t)\ \mathrm{d}t}{\int_{0}^{1}m(t)\ \mathrm{d}t}.

Then, the choice

\varphi\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\tau\mathbin{\circ}h\mathbin{\cdot}\mathord{\mathbbm{1}_{\mathord{\mathbb{U}(o,2)}}}

yields a smooth function with the desired properties.

such that
\varphi=1
on
\mathord{\mathbb{U}(o,1)}
and
\varphi=0
on
\mathord{\mathbb{U}(o,2)}
.
Define
\varphi_{n}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\varphi(\nicefrac{{\mathord{\mathrm{Id}}}}{{n}})
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
.
Note that there is some constant
c\in(0,\infty)
such that

\lvert\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}\varphi_{n}(x)\rvert_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}\leq\frac{c}{n}\,\quad\text{and}\quad\lvert\operatorname{Hess}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}\varphi_{n}(x)\rvert_{\infty}\leq\frac{c}{n^{2}}\,

for all
x\in\mathord{\mathbb{M}}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
,
cf.
[
GS16
, Definition 3.3]
.
Clearly,
(\varphi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
pointwisely converges to the constant function 1 as
n\to\infty
.
Let
f_{0}\in\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\right\rparen}

and define

(3.4)

f_{n}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}\mathbin{\otimes}\varphi_{n}^{\mathrm{h}}=f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}\mathbin{\cdot}\varphi_{n}^{\mathrm{h}}\qquad\text{for all }n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}.

This yields a sequence in
D
converging to
f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}
in
H
.
From dominated convergence we can conclude that

Sf_{n}=\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\,f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}\mathbin{\cdot}\mathop{\Delta_{\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\varphi_{n}^{\mathrm{h}}-\alpha f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}\mathbin{\cdot}\mathcal{V}\varphi_{n}^{\mathrm{h}}\longrightarrow 0\qquad\text{in }H\text{ as }n\to\infty,

where we use
that the function

\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\to\mathbb{R},\,v\mapsto\lvert\mathcal{V}(v)\rvert_{\mathord{\mathrm{s}}}=\lvert v\rvert_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}

is in
H

as on each fibre
\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{x}}{\textstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{\text{n}}

the function

\lvert\mathord{\mathrm{Id}}_{\mathbb{R}^{\text{n}}}\rvert_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}

is in
\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathbb{R}^{\text{n}};\nu\right\rparen}
.
Since
\mathord{\left\lparen S,\mathord{D\left\lparen S\right\rparen}\right\rparen}
is closed,
we have shown that
f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}\in\mathord{D\left\lparen S\right\rparen}
, and more specifically
Sf_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}=0
.
Next, we prove that
P
maps into the null space of
\mathord{\left\lparen S,\mathord{D\left\lparen S\right\rparen}\right\rparen}
.
As the range of
P
is contained in
\pi_{0}^{\ast}\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}};\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}\right\rparen}
,
we pick an
g_{0}\in\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}};\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}\right\rparen}

and show that
g_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}\in\mathord{D\left\lparen S\right\rparen}
and
Sg_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}=0
.
The space
\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\right\rparen}
is dense in
\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}};\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}\right\rparen}
,
so there is a sequence
(g_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}}
in
\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\right\rparen}

approximating
g_{0}
in
\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}};\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}\right\rparen}
.
We have seen just before
that
(g_{n}^{\mathrm{v}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}}
is a sequence in
\mathord{D\left\lparen S\right\rparen}

and
Sg_{n}^{\mathrm{v}}=0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Again, with closedness of
\mathord{\left\lparen S,\mathord{D\left\lparen S\right\rparen}\right\rparen}

it follows that
g_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}\in\mathord{D\left\lparen S\right\rparen}
and
Sg_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}=0
.

Now, we turn to the statements involving
A
.
Let
f_{0}\in\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\right\rparen}

and define the sequence
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}}
as in (
3.4
)
approximating
f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}
in
H
.
Using

\lvert\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}\rvert_{\mathord{\mathrm{s}}}=\lvert\mathord{\mathrm{Id}}_{\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}\rvert_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}\in H

as above and that

\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}\Phi\in\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}};\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}\right\rparen}

we can infer via dominated convergence that

\displaystyle Af_{n}

\displaystyle=-\varphi_{n}^{\mathrm{h}}\mathbin{\cdot}\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}+\frac{1}{\beta}f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}\mathbin{\cdot}\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\Phi^{\mathrm{h}}\right\rparen\varphi_{n}^{\mathrm{h}}=-\varphi_{n}^{\mathrm{h}}\mathbin{\cdot}\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}+\frac{1}{\beta}f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}\mathbin{\cdot}\mathord{\mathrm{m}\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}\Phi\mathbin{,}\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}\varphi_{n}\right\rparen}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow-\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}+\frac{1}{\beta}f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}\mathbin{\cdot}0\qquad\text{in }H\text{ as }n\to\infty.

Closedness of
\mathord{\left\lparen A,\mathord{D\left\lparen A\right\rparen}\right\rparen}
implies
f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}\in\mathord{D\left\lparen A\right\rparen}
as well as

Af_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}=-\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}
.
For the inclusion
P(D)\subseteq\mathord{D\left\lparen A\right\rparen}

it’s enough to show that
1\in\mathord{D\left\lparen A\right\rparen}
and
A1=0
.
Similar as before the function

\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\to\mathbb{R},\,v\mapsto\lvert\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}(v)\rvert_{\mathord{\mathrm{s}}}=\lvert v\rvert_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}

is in
H
,
and thus dominated convergence gives us

A\varphi_{n}^{\mathrm{v}}=-\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}\varphi_{n}^{\mathrm{v}}\longrightarrow 0\qquad\text{in }H\text{ as }n\to\infty.

In order to prove now that
AP(D)\subseteq\mathord{D\left\lparen A\right\rparen}

we adhere to our approximation strategy and define

h_{n}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\varphi_{n}^{\mathrm{h}}\mathbin{\cdot}\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
.
The sequence
(h_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}

both pointwisely and in
H
.
We note that the function

\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}^{2}f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}=\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}})

is dominated by

\lVert\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}\rVert_{\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\to\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}};\mu\right\rparen}}\mathbin{\cdot}\lVert\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}\rVert_{H}\leq\lVert\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}\rVert_{\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\to\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}};\mu\right\rparen}}^{2}\mathbin{\cdot}\lVert f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}\rVert_{H}=\lVert\lvert\mathord{\mathrm{Id}}_{\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}\rvert_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}\rVert_{H}^{2}\mathbin{\cdot}\lVert f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}\rVert_{H}

due to the Cauchy-Bunyakovsky-Schwarz inequality applied twice.
This dominating function is in
H

as on each fibre
\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{x}}{\textstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{x}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{\text{n}}

the function

\lvert\mathord{\mathrm{Id}}_{\mathbb{R}^{\text{n}}}\rvert^{2}

is in
\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathbb{R}^{\text{n}};\nu\right\rparen}
.
Together with afore-mentioned facts that

\lvert\mathord{\mathrm{Id}}_{\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}\rvert_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}\in H

and

\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}\Phi\in\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}};\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}\right\rparen}

this yields

\displaystyle Ah_{n}

\displaystyle=-\varphi_{n}^{\mathrm{h}}\mathbin{\cdot}\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}^{2}f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}+\frac{1}{\beta}\mathbin{\cdot}\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\Phi^{\mathrm{h}}\right\rparen h_{n}

\displaystyle=-\varphi_{n}^{\mathrm{h}}\mathbin{\cdot}\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}^{2}f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}+\frac{1}{\beta}\mathbin{\cdot}\left\lparen\left\lparen\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}\right\rparen\mathbin{\cdot}\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\Phi^{\mathrm{h}}\right\rparen\varphi_{n}^{\mathrm{h}}+\varphi_{n}^{\mathrm{h}}\mathbin{\cdot}\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\Phi^{\mathrm{h}}\right\rparen(\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}})\right\rparen

\displaystyle\longrightarrow-\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}^{2}f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}+0+\frac{1}{\beta}\mathbin{\cdot}\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{v}}}}\Phi^{\mathrm{h}}\right\rparen(\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}})\qquad\text{in }H\text{ as }n\to\infty

by dominated convergence.
Since
\mathord{\left\lparen A,\mathord{D\left\lparen A\right\rparen}\right\rparen}
is closed,
the function
\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}
is an element of
\mathord{D\left\lparen A\right\rparen}
.

Finally, the statements on
L
follow the very same way:
Let
f_{0}\in\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\right\rparen}

and define the sequence
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}}
as in (
3.4
);
repeat the previous steps and conclude from closedness of
\mathord{\left\lparen L,\mathord{D\left\lparen L\right\rparen}\right\rparen}

that
f=f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}\in\mathord{D\left\lparen L\right\rparen}
and
Lf_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}=-Af_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}
.
In particular, the sequence

\left\lparen L\varphi_{n}^{\mathrm{v}}\right\rparen_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

converges in
H
to 0 as
n\to\infty
,
and by closedness it follows

1\in\mathord{D\left\lparen L\right\rparen}
with
L1=0
.
∎

Remark 3.9
.

As we deduced
Pf\in\mathord{D\left\lparen A\right\rparen}
for all
f\in D
,
we can calculate that

(3.5)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} APf&=-\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}}\left\lparen\operatorname{E}_{\nu}[f]^{\mathrm{v}}\right\rparen=-\mathord{\left\langle\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}\mathbin{,}d\operatorname{E}_{\nu}[f]\mathbin{\circ}d\pi_{0}\right\rangle}\\
&=-\mathord{\left\langle\mathord{\mathrm{Id}}_{\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}\mathbin{,}d\operatorname{E}_{\nu}[f]\right\rangle}=-d\operatorname{E}_{\nu}[f].\end{aligned}

However, one can give this formula a more intuitive form:

(3.6)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} APf&=-\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}}\left\lparen Pf\right\rparen=-\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}\left\lparen\operatorname{E}_{\nu}[f]^{\mathrm{v}}\right\rparen=-\mathord{\mathrm{h}\left\lparen\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}\mathbin{,}\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{h}}}}(P_{S}f)\right\rparen}\\
&=-\mathord{\mathrm{h}\left\lparen\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}\mathbin{,}\horlift\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}\operatorname{E}_{\nu}[f]\right\rparen\right\rparen}=-\mathord{\mathrm{m}_{\pi_{0}}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{Id}}_{\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}\mathbin{,}\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}\operatorname{E}_{\nu}[f]\mathbin{\circ}\pi_{0}\right\rparen}.\end{aligned}

Unlike
Equation (
3.5
)
,
we recognise that
Equation (
3.6
)

is in perfect correspondence to
[
GS16
, Equation (3.12)]
.
∎

Turning to the remaining condition
(D2)
, i. e. the question whether
\mathord{\left\lparen L,\mathord{D\left\lparen L\right\rparen}\right\rparen}
generates a strongly continuous semigroup,
we first look into the case of a smooth potential
\Psi\in\mathord{C^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\right\rparen}
.
The proof relies on methods from
[
HN05
]

as explained in
[
GS14
, Section 4]
.
We briefly quote a consequence of the Hörmander Theorem,
namely
[
HN05
, Proposition A.1]
.
Let
T
be a second order differential operator
on a Riemannian manifold
(\mathord{\mathbb{B}},\mathord{\mathrm{b}})

of the form
T=c+\mathcal{X}_{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}\mathcal{X}_{k}

with
c\in\mathord{C^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{B}}\right\rparen}

and
\mathcal{X}_{k}\in\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\textstyle\Gamma^{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}{\scriptstyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma^{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}\right\rparen}
for all
k\in\{0,\ldots,\ell\}
.
We say that
T
satisfies the
Hörmander condition

if at any point
b\in\mathord{\mathbb{B}}
holds

\dim(\mathord{\mathrm{Lie}_{b}\left\lparen\mathcal{X}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{X}_{\ell}\right\rparen})=\dim(\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{b}}{\textstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle b$}}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{b}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle b$}}}\mathord{\mathbb{B}}})=\dim(\mathord{\mathbb{B}}),

where
\mathord{\mathrm{Lie}\left\lparen\mathcal{X}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{X}_{\ell}\right\rparen}
denotes the generated Lie algebra.

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
T
satisfy the Hörmander condition
and let
f\in\mathord{L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{B}};\mathord{\mathrm{b}}\right\rparen}
such that

\int_{\mathord{\mathbb{B}}}f\mathbin{\cdot}T\psi\ \mathrm{d}\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{b}}}}=0\qquad\text{for all }\psi\in\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{B}}\right\rparen}.

Then,
f
has a smooth representative.

Proof.

The proof of this proposition works as for
[
GS14
, Proposition A.1]
:
It is done in chart domains,
and within these domains it’s perfectly fine to consider

\mathcal{X}_{j}=\partial x^{j}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}
,
where
\left\lparen x^{j}\right\rparen_{j=1}^{\dim(\mathord{\mathbb{B}})}
denotes local coordinates provided by the chart.
Then, we have smooth representatives in chart domains
serving as starting point for a partition of unity argument.
Thus, even if
T
is just available in local coordinate form,
we can apply the previous proposition
as soon as the respective chart domains form an open cover.
∎

Lemma 3.11

(Hörmander condition for the Langevin generator)
.

Consider a smooth potential
\Psi\in\mathord{C^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\right\rparen}
.
Then, the Langevin generator satisfies the Hörmander condition.
More precisely, let
v\in\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}

and
\left\lparen x^{j}\right\rparen_{j=1}^{\text{n}}
be local coordinates corresponding to a chart at
\pi_{0}(v)
.
Then, we have that

\displaystyle\dim\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{Lie}_{v}\left\lparen\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}},\vertlift\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}\Psi\right\rparen,\vertlift\left\lparen\partial x^{1}\right\rparen,\ldots,\vertlift\left\lparen\partial x^{\text{n}}\right\rparen,\mathcal{V}\right\rparen}\right\rparen

\displaystyle=\dim(\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{v}}{\textstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle v$}}}{\scriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{v}}{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{T}_{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle v$}}}\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}})

\displaystyle=\dim(\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}})=2\text{n}.

Proof.

Obviously, neither
\vertlift\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}\Psi\right\rparen
nor
\mathcal{V}
contribute anything to the generated Lie algebra.
From
[
GK02
, Proposition 5.1]
we know explicit forms
for the Lie brackets of vertically and horizontally lifted vector fields in any combination.
With that said, we have the equations

\mathord{\left[\vertlift(\mathcal{X})\mathbin{,}\vertlift(\mathcal{Y})\right]}=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathord{\left[\horlift(\mathcal{Y})\mathbin{,}\vertlift(\mathcal{X})\right]}=\vertlift\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}\mathcal{X}\right\rparen

for all
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}\in\mathord{\mathchoice{\displaystyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\textstyle\Gamma^{\raise 0.60275pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}{\scriptstyle\Gamma^{\infty}}{\scriptscriptstyle\Gamma^{\raise-0.21529pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\infty$}}}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}\right\rparen}
.
Thus, the only nontrivial pairing of the seeding vector fields

\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}\in\{\partial x^{1},\ldots,\partial x^{\text{n}}\}

is

\mathord{\left[\horlift(\mathcal{Y})\mathbin{,}\vertlift(\mathcal{X})\right]}=\vertlift\left\lparen\mathord{\nabla_{\mathcal{Y}}^{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}\mathcal{X}\right\rparen
,
but clearly all vector fields of this form are linear dependent of the vertical vector fields

\left\{\vertlift\left\lparen\partial x^{1}\right\rparen,\ldots,\vertlift\left\lparen\partial x^{\text{n}}\right\rparen\right\}

generating the Lie algebra.
This just shows that the collection

\{\partial x^{1},\ldots,\partial x^{\text{n}}\}

might not serve our purpose
even if we would build our generator with both kinds of liftings.

The statement concerning Lie brackets of one vertically and one horizontally lifted vector fields
does not apply to
\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}
,
as a semispray can not arise as horizontal lift of a vector field.
However, in
Lemma
A.1

we prove that

\mathord{\left[\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}\mathbin{,}\vertlift\left\lparen\partial x^{j}\right\rparen\right]}=\horlift\left\lparen\partial x^{j}\right\rparen-\sum_{i=1}^{\text{n}}N_{j}^{i}\mathbin{\cdot}\vertlift\left\lparen\partial x^{i}\right\rparen

for certain functions
N_{j}^{i}
.
This yields
n
many linear independent horizontal vector fields in the generated Lie algebra
which finishes the proof.
∎

Theorem 3.12

(
(D2)
for smooth potentials using a hypoellipticity startegy)
.

Let
\Psi\in\mathord{C^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\right\rparen}
be a smooth potential.
Then,
(L,D)
is essentially m-dissipative.
Thus, its closure
\mathord{\left\lparen L,\mathord{D\left\lparen L\right\rparen}\right\rparen}
generates a strongly continuous semigroup.

Proof.

From
Lemma
3.4
we know that

(L,D)
is dissipative.
In view of the Lumer-Philips Theorem,
we have to show that
the range
(\mathord{\mathrm{Id}}_{H}-L)(D)
is dense in
H
.
Let
f\in H
fixed such that

(3.7)

\left\lparen(\mathord{\mathrm{Id}}_{H}-L)u\mathbin{,}f\right\rparen_{H}=0\qquad\text{for all }u\in D.

We claim that
f=0
.

Due to the choice of
f
we have that

\exp(-\Phi^{\mathrm{v}})f\in\mathord{L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}};\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}\nu\right\rparen}

and by
Lemma
3.11

we can assume that
\exp(-\Phi^{\mathrm{v}})f
is smooth.
Let
(\varphi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of cut-off functions in
\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}};[0,1]\right\rparen}

with
\varphi_{n}\rightarrow 1
pointwise as
n\to\infty

and such that

\lVert\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}\varphi_{n}\rVert_{\mathord{L^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}};\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}\right\rparen}}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{n}\,C

holds for all
n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
and some
C\in(0,\infty)
.
Now, define
u_{n}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\left\lparen\varphi_{n}^{\mathrm{v}}\right\rparen^{2}f
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
It’s clear that

\left\lparen u_{n}\mathbin{,}f\right\rparen_{H}\overset{\eqref{eq:aux:hypoellip-D2}}{=}\left\lparen Lu_{n}\mathbin{,}f\right\rparen_{H}=\left\lparen Su_{n}\mathbin{,}f\right\rparen_{H}-\left\lparen Au_{n}\mathbin{,}f\right\rparen_{H}.

Due to the fact that
(S,D)
is nonpositive definite and multiplying with
\varphi
commutes with the action of
S

we see that

\left\lparen Su_{n}\mathbin{,}f\right\rparen_{H}=\left\lparen S(\varphi_{n}^{\mathrm{v}}f)\mathbin{,}\varphi_{n}^{\mathrm{v}}f\right\rparen_{H}\leq 0
.
Similarly, using antisymmetry we get that

\left\lparen Au_{n}\mathbin{,}f\right\rparen_{H}=\left\lparen A\left\lparen\varphi_{n}^{\mathrm{v}}\right\rparen\,\varphi_{n}^{\mathrm{v}}f\mathbin{,}f\right\rparen_{H}+\left\lparen A(\varphi_{n}^{\mathrm{v}}f)\mathbin{,}\varphi_{n}^{\mathrm{v}}f\right\rparen_{H}=\left\lparen A\left\lparen\varphi_{n}^{\mathrm{v}}\right\rparen\,\varphi_{n}^{\mathrm{v}}f\mathbin{,}f\right\rparen_{H}.

Altogether, this yields the estimate

\left\lparen u_{n}\mathbin{,}f\right\rparen_{H}=\int_{\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}\left\lparen\varphi_{n}^{\mathrm{v}}\right\rparen^{2}f^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\mu\leq\frac{1}{n}\,C\int_{\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}\varphi_{n}^{\mathrm{v}}f^{2}\ \mathrm{d}\mu\leq\frac{1}{n}\,C\lVert f\rVert_{H}

using (
3.6
).
By dominated convergence this implies that

\lVert f\rVert_{H}^{2}\leq 0
,
thus
f=0
.
∎

For the case of a loc-Lipschitzian potential,
we leave the base weight aside for a moment,
but keep the fibre weight.
In other words,
we could think of
\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}
endowed with bundle weight
1\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}\rho_{F}
.
Let

\Psi\in\mathord{L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}};\mathord{\mathrm{m}}\right\rparen}
,
and

L_{0}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\,\mathop{\Delta_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}}}+\,\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}

be defined on the very same set
D_{0}
as before –
it should be clear at this point that

L_{0}
has to consist of the weighted diffusion and the
non
-corrected semispray
instead of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}}
.

Lemma 3.13
.

(L_{0},D_{0})
is essentially m-dissipative on
\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}};\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}\nu\right\rparen}
.

Proof.

From
Theorem 3.12
applied to the smooth case of the zero potential,
we know that

(L_{0},D)
is essentially m-dissipative
on
\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}};\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}\nu\right\rparen}
.
We have to show that

(L_{0},D)
is contained in the closure of
(L_{0},D_{0})
.

For any
f\in D
there is an approximating sequence
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
D_{0}

wrt. usual locally convex topology implying uniform convergence of all derivatives on compacts
cf.
Lemma
2.40
.
Furthermore, there is a common compact set in
\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}
large enough
containing
\supp(f)
and
\supp(f_{n})
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Therefore, we have that

\sup_{v\in\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}\lvert L_{0}f_{n}(v)-L_{0}f(v)\rvert\rightarrow 0

as
n\to\infty
.
Thus,
f_{n}\rightarrow f
and
L_{0}f_{n}\rightarrow L_{0}f

in
\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}};\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}\nu\right\rparen}
as
n\to\infty
.
∎

Denote by
\mathord{H_{0}^{1,\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\right\rparen}

the closure of
\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\right\rparen}
wrt.
\mathord{H^{1,\infty}}
-
norm.
Define

D_{1}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=\pi_{0}^{\ast}\mathord{H_{0}^{1,\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\right\rparen}\mathbin{\otimes}\kappa^{\ast}\mathord{C_{c}^{\infty}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathbb{M}}\right\rparen}.

Considering the operator
(L,D_{1})
we realise that

Lemma
3.4
still does apply –
the proof has to be adapted just slightly.
Further on, we define the unitary isomorphism

U\colon\ H=\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}};\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{m}}}}}\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}\nu\right\rparen}\to\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}};\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}\nu\right\rparen},\ f\longmapsto\exp\left\lparen-\nicefrac{{\Phi}}{{2}}\right\rparen^{\mathrm{v}}\mathbin{\cdot}f.

Note that
U(D_{1})=D_{1}
.
Thus, we define the operator
\widetilde{L}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=ULU^{-1}
on
D_{1}
.
One directly observes that

\widetilde{L}=\widetilde{S}-\widetilde{A}\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}\hss}\raisebox{-1.29167pt}{$\cdot$}}=U\mathop{\Delta_{\mathord{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{v}}}}}U^{-1}-UAU^{-1}\qquad\text{holds on }D_{1}

with operators
(\widetilde{S},D_{1})
symmetric, negative semidefinite
and
(\widetilde{A},D_{1})
antisymmetric
both on
\mathord{L^{2}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}};\mathord{\lambda_{\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}\mathbin{\otimes_{\mathrm{loc}}}\nu\right\rparen}
,
as well as

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}

\displaystyle=-U\left\lparen f_{0}^{\mathrm{v}}\mathbin{\cdot}\mathord{\mathrm{m}_{\pi_{0}}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{Id}}_{\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}\mathbin{,}\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}\exp(\nicefrac{{\Phi}}{{2}})\mathbin{\circ}\pi_{0}\right\rparen}+\exp(\nicefrac{{\Phi}}{{2}})^{\mathrm{v}}\mathbin{\cdot}\mathord{\mathrm{m}_{\pi_{0}}\left\lparen\mathord{\mathrm{Id}}_{\mathord{\mathrm{T}\mathord{\mathbb{M}}}}\mathbin{,}\mathord{\nabla_{\mkern-3.0mu\mathord{\mathrm{m}}}}f_{0}\mathbin{\circ}\pi_{0}\right\rparen}\right\rparen
